zero or negative, and both possibilities are explicitly covered. The
factorization certificate is finite even when the element being factored
is surreal.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Dyadic bracketing is inductive without a character}\label{isg:sc:sec:inductive}

\begin{lemma}[Doubling and successor]\label{isg:sc:lem:successor}
In every discretely ordered ring satisfying $\ED_2$,
\[
 \PowDy(1),\qquad
 (\forall y)(\PowDy(y)\to\PowDy(2y)),\qquad
 \StdDy(0),\qquad
 (\forall x)(\StdDy(x)\to\StdDy(x+1))
\]
hold in the positive cone.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A positive product $(2u+3)v$ is at least $3$, proving $\PowDy(1)$.
Suppose $\PowDy(y)$, but $2y=(2u+3)v$ for nonnegative bounded witnesses.
Reduction modulo $2$ shows that $v$ is even: the standard remainder map
$R\to\Z/2\Z$ is a ring homomorphism, and the odd factor has remainder $1$.
Write $v=2w$. Cancellation gives $y=(2u+3)w$. Since $y>0$, one has
$w\ge1$, and therefore $u\le y$ and $w\le y$. This contradicts
$\PowDy(y)$.

The assertion $\StdDy(0)$ is immediate, and $y=1$ witnesses $\StdDy(1)$.
For $x>0$, let $y\le x<2y$ witness $\StdDy(x)$. Discreteness gives
$x+1\le2y$. If $x+1<2y$, reuse $y$. If $x+1=2y$, use $2y$ as the new
witness, invoking closure of $\PowDy$ under doubling. Its positivity gives
$x+1=2y<4y$. In either case $\StdDy(x+1)$ follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A single induction sentence detects standardness]\label{isg:sc:thm:boundary}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}, the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},nosep]
\item $R$ is its standard copy of $\Z$;
\item $R_+\models\forall x\,\StdDy(x)$;
\item $R_+$ satisfies the one sentence
\begin{equation}\label{isg:sc:eq:oneind}
\bigl(\StdDy(0)\wedge\forall x(\StdDy(x)\to\StdDy(x+1))\bigr)
       \longrightarrow\forall x\,\StdDy(x);
\end{equation}
\item $R_+$ is a model of $\Idelta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}, (ii) says exactly that every nonnegative
element is standard. Negating negative elements then gives (i). By
\cref{isg:sc:lem:successor}, the antecedent in \eqref{isg:sc:eq:oneind}
always holds, so (ii) and (iii) are equivalent. Since $\StdDy$ is bounded,
(iv) implies (iii), while (i) implies (iv) because ordinary arithmetic
satisfies induction.
\end{proof}

Write $\Ind(\StdDy)$ for the sentence \eqref{isg:sc:eq:oneind}. The
formula $\StdDy$ has bounded existential--universal form after expanding
its abbreviations: one bounded existential witness followed by two
bounded universal witnesses. No minimality claim about this quantifier
pattern is made. The result identifies an explicit boundary, rather than
only invoking a known strict inclusion between induction fragments. Any
parameter-free formula that defines exactly $\N$ in every ring of this
class, such as the four-square formula of \cite[Theorem~2.9]{ELV}
(\cref{isg:sc:rem:ELV}), gives the same equivalence within the class;
what is particular to $\StdDy$ is that its antecedent holds by
\cref{isg:sc:lem:successor} in \emph{every} ring with $\ED_2$, whether or
not it has a character. \Cref{isg:sc:cor:profiles} uses exactly that.

\subsection{Why both small divisors occur}\label{isg:sc:sec:sharp}
The assumptions $\ED_2$ and $\ED_3$ cannot simply be omitted from
\cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic} while leaving the detector unchanged. For a prime
$p$, set
\[
 R_{(p)}=\Z+X\Z[1/p][X],
\]
ordered by the sign of the leading coefficient with $X$ positive infinite.
These are discretely ordered rings with the character $f\mapsto f(0)$, and
they satisfy $\ED_p$.

\begin{proposition}[Two sharpness examples]\label{isg:sc:prop:sharp}
In $R_{(2)}$, the positive infinite element $X$ satisfies $\PowDy$. In
$R_{(3)}$, the positive infinite element $X+1$ satisfies $\PowDy$. Thus
division by $2$ alone, or by $3$ alone, does not suffice.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $X=(2u+3)v$ in $R_{(2)}$ with $u,v\ge0$. Both factors
are nonzero, so polynomial degrees show that one is constant. If $2u+3$ is
constant, it is an ordinary odd integer $d\ge3$. Then $v=X/d$, impossible
because $1/d\notin\Z[1/2]$. If $v$ is constant, it is a positive integer;
comparing constant terms gives $0=(2u(0)+3)v$, impossible since the first
factor is odd.

Next suppose $X+1=(2u+3)v$ in $R_{(3)}$. If the odd factor is constant
$d\ge3$, the constant term of $v$ would be $1/d$, not an integer. If $v$
is constant, comparison of constant terms forces $v=1$ and $2u(0)+3=1$;
comparison of the coefficient of $X$ then forces a coefficient $1/2$ in
$u$, which is not in $\Z[1/3]$. These contradictions do not even need the
additional bounds $u,v\le y$.
\end{proof}

These examples concern the specific dyadic detector, not the existence of
some other standard-cut definition. That distinction matters in view of
the more general definability methods in the literature. \textup{[}The
write adds: both rings carry a unital ring retraction onto $\Z$, so the
four-square formula of \cite[Theorem~2.9]{ELV} defines $\N$ in each of
them (\cref{isg:sc:rem:ELV}).\textup{]}

\section{Integer characters, bounded arithmetic and initial realizability}\label{isg:sc:sec:characters}

\subsection{Classification of integer characters}
\begin{theorem}[Classification of integer characters]\label{isg:sc:thm:characters}
Let $R$ be a discretely ordered ring with standard division. Exactly one
of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},nosep]
\item $\Hom_{\Z}(R_{\rm add},\Z)=0$;
\item there is a unique normalized integer character $\chi$, it is a
unital ring homomorphism, and
\[
 \Hom_{\Z}(R_{\rm add},\Z)=\{k\chi:k\in\Z\}.
\]
\end{enumerate}
In the second case, with $R^{\dv}=\ker\chi$, one has
\begin{equation}\label{isg:sc:eq:kernel}
 R^{\dv}=\bigcap_{n\ge1}nR,\qquad R_{\rm add}=\Z\oplus R^{\dv},
 \qquad R^{\dv}\text{ is a divisible additive group and an ideal}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $f:R_{\rm add}\to\Z$ be additive, and put $k=f(1)$. If $k=0$, then
$a=nq+r$ implies $f(a)=nf(q)$ for every standard $n$. An ordinary integer
divisible by every positive integer is zero, so $f=0$.

If $k\ne0$, division by $|k|$ gives $f(a)=|k|f(q)+rk$, so every value of
$f$ is divisible by $k$ in $\Z$. Hence $\chi(a)=f(a)/k$ is an
integer-valued additive map and $\chi(1)=1$. For any normalized character,
\cref{isg:sc:lem:residue} shows that $\chi(a)$ is congruent modulo each
standard $n$ to the standard remainder of $a$. Two normalized characters
must therefore agree at every $a$.

For multiplication, let $a$ and $b$ have remainders $r$ and $s$ modulo
$n$. The remainder of $ab$ is congruent to $rs$, so
\[
 \chi(ab)\equiv rs\equiv\chi(a)\chi(b)\pmod n.
\]
As this holds for every standard $n$, the two ordinary integers are equal.
Thus $\chi$ is multiplicative. Uniqueness and the normalization argument
now give the asserted description of all $f$.

If $\chi(a)=0$, division by $n$ has remainder zero and gives $a=nq$.
Moreover $0=n\chi(q)$, so $q\in R^{\dv}$. This proves that $R^{\dv}$ is
divisible and contained in every $nR$. Conversely, membership in every
$nR$ makes $\chi(a)$ divisible by every $n$, hence zero. Finally,
$a=\chi(a)+(a-\chi(a))$ is the unique additive decomposition into a
standard integer and an element of $R^{\dv}$. Multiplicativity makes
$R^{\dv}$ an ideal.
\end{proof}

In fact, multiplicativity of a normalized character already follows from
$\ED_2$: iterate division to obtain remainders modulo $2^j$, and use that
an ordinary integer divisible by every $2^j$ is zero
(\cref{isg:sc:prop:repair}). Standard division is retained in
\cref{isg:sc:thm:characters} because it also normalizes an arbitrary
nonzero character and gives the all-moduli formulation.

For the $\Z$-group $(R,+)$, case (ii) is the split case of
\cref{isg:cf:prop:residuesplit}: $\chi$ is the projection onto $\Z$ along
the unique complement $R^{\dv}=G^{\dv}$, and \eqref{isg:sc:eq:kernel} is
\cref{isg:cf:lem:divkernel} together with condition (iii) there. What the
ring structure adds is that this projection is automatically a ring
homomorphism and $R^{\dv}$ an ideal. \textup{[}This comparison is the
write's; source~03 does not cite this report.\textup{]}

\subsection{Profinite residues do not distinguish the rings by themselves}\label{isg:sc:sec:residues}
Standard division and discreteness identify $R/nR$ with $\Z/n\Z$ for every
ordinary $n\ge1$, compatibly when one modulus divides another. Thus there
is a canonical unital ring map
\[
 \res_R:R\longrightarrow\Zhat=\varprojlim_{n\ge1}\Z/n\Z.
\]
Here the inverse system is indexed by divisibility. This is the
ordinary-modulus completion; no topology on $R$ has been assumed. On the
additive group it is the coherent residue $\res_G$ of
\cref{isg:cf:lem:residues} for $G=(R,+)$ and $e=1$.

\begin{proposition}[The diagonal-image criterion]\label{isg:sc:prop:diagonal}
For a ring with standard division, a normalized integer character exists
if and only if $\im\res_R$ is contained in the diagonal copy of $\Z$ in
$\Zhat$. In that case $\im\res_R=\Z$ and $\res_R=\iota\circ\chi$, where
$\iota:\Z\hookrightarrow\Zhat$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A character supplies exactly the standard remainders, proving one
implication. Conversely, if $\res_R(a)=\iota(m)$ for some integer $m$,
that integer is unique. Define $\chi(a)=m$. Because $\res_R$ and $\iota$
preserve the ring operations and $\iota$ is injective, so does $\chi$. The
image contains every standard integer, proving equality rather than just
inclusion.
\end{proof}
For the additive group this is the equivalence (i)$\Leftrightarrow$(ii)
of \cref{isg:cf:prop:residuesplit}, in its ring version.

Every nonzero finite unital quotient of $R$ is also an ordinary cyclic
ring: its finite characteristic is some $n\ge2$, and each element of $R$
is congruent modulo $nR$ to a standard integer. It follows that the
quotient is generated by its unit and is isomorphic to $\Z/n\Z$, where $n$
is its characteristic. Therefore neither these finite quotient rings nor
the abstract inverse limit alone separate omnific arithmetic from
nonstandard bounded arithmetic. The canonical image in the inverse limit
is the extra information that matters, as for $\Z$-groups after
\cref{isg:cf:main:presburger}.

\subsection{No unit-preserving additive map from bounded arithmetic}\label{isg:sc:sec:nogo}
For a model $M$ of $\PAminus$, its \emph{difference ring} $R_M$ is the
group completion under addition, equipped with the usual induced
multiplication and order. Its nonnegative cone is naturally $M$, and its
additive group is the ordered additive group completion $G(M)$ of
\cref{isg:cf:thm:PA}.

\begin{lemma}[Standard division in bounded arithmetic]\label{isg:sc:lem:idiv}
If $M\models\Idelta$, its difference ring has standard division.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix a standard $n\ge1$. In $M$, use bounded induction on $a$ for
\[
 (\exists q\le a)(\exists r<n)\ a=nq+r.
\]
The base case is $q=r=0$. For the successor, increment $r$ if $r+1<n$;
otherwise $r=n-1$, so replace $(q,r)$ by $(q+1,0)$. The witnesses remain
bounded by $a+1$. This proves division for nonnegative elements. If
$b=nq+r\ge0$, then $-b=n(-q)+0$ when $r=0$, and $-b=n(-q-1)+(n-r)$ when
$r>0$. Thus division holds in $R_M$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Vanishing of integer characters]\label{isg:sc:thm:nochar}
If $M$ is a nonstandard model of $\Idelta$, then
\begin{equation}\label{isg:sc:eq:nochar}
 \Hom_{\Z}((R_M)_{\rm add},\Z)=0.
\end{equation}
In particular, the inclusion of standard integers into $(R_M)_{\rm add}$
does not split as an inclusion of abelian groups.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{isg:sc:lem:idiv}, standard division holds. A nonzero character
would give a normalized character by \cref{isg:sc:thm:characters}. The
formula $\StdDy$ would then define exactly the standard natural numbers by
\cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}. But \cref{isg:sc:lem:successor} makes that
formula inductive, and $\Idelta$ contains its induction instance. It would
follow that every element of $M$ is standard, a contradiction. A
splitting would itself be a normalized integer character.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A second route through the four-square formula; \textup{[write]}]\label{isg:sc:rem:ELV}
A discretely ordered ring $R$ with a unital ring homomorphism
$\chi:R\to\Z$ is an ordered Dorroh ring in the sense of
\cite[Section~2.5]{ELV}, with witnessing ideal $\ker\chi$: the quotient is
$\Z$, and a positive element of $\ker\chi$ is not a standard integer, so
it is positive infinite by \cref{isg:sc:lem:finite}, as their ordered
condition requires. By \cref{isg:sc:prop:repair} a normalized character of
a ring with $\ED_2$ is such a homomorphism. Hence
\cite[Theorem~2.9]{ELV} supplies, in every ring of
\cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}, a second parameter-free $\Delta_0$ definition of
$\N$ in $R_+$, and the proof of \cref{isg:sc:thm:nochar} goes through
with it in place of $\StdDy$: a nonzero character is normalized and
multiplicative by \cref{isg:sc:thm:characters}, the four-square formula
then defines $\N$ in $M$, $\N$ is closed under $0$ and successor, and its
$\Delta_0$ induction instance makes $M$ standard. The second route still
needs \cref{isg:sc:thm:characters}; it replaces only the detector. It does
not give \cref{isg:sc:cor:profiles}, which uses the inductiveness of
$\StdDy$ in a ring \emph{without} a character. Source~03 credits
\cite[Section~2.5]{ELV} in general terms; this comparison with its
Theorem~2.9 is the write's, from the v2 text of \cite{ELV}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The additive-map obstruction]\label{isg:sc:cor:noadd}
Let $\Asc$ be a unital ring with a unital ring retraction
$\chi_{\Asc}:\Asc\to\Z$, and let $M\models\Idelta$ be nonstandard. Every
additive map $f:R_M\to\Asc$ satisfies
\[
 \im f\subseteq\ker\chi_{\Asc}.
\]
There is no additive map $f:M\to\Asc$ preserving $0$, addition, and $1$.
Consequently there is no unital ring homomorphism $R_M\to\Asc$ and no
unital semiring homomorphism $M\to\Asc$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The composite $\chi_{\Asc}f$ is an additive map to $\Z$, so it is zero by
\cref{isg:sc:thm:nochar}. A unit-preserving additive map on $M$ extends
to $R_M$ by $f(a-b)=f(a)-f(b)$; cancellation verifies that this is
well-defined. Its composite with $\chi_{\Asc}$ would take $1$ to $1$,
which is impossible. The multiplicative statements are special cases.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Even nonunital ring maps vanish]\label{isg:sc:cor:nonunital}
If, additionally, $\Asc$ is a domain, then every ring homomorphism
$R_M\to\Asc$, without a requirement to preserve $1$, is zero.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The image of $1$ is an idempotent, hence $0$ or $1$ in the domain $\Asc$.
The second option is excluded by \cref{isg:sc:cor:noadd}. In the first
option, $f(a)=f(1a)=f(1)f(a)=0$ for every $a$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Existence, not regularity]\label{isg:sc:rem:regularity}
None of these maps is assumed continuous, Borel, definable, computable,
injective, or order-preserving. The obstruction concerns the existence of
a map, not its regularity. It is therefore complementary to Glazer's
result, not a strengthening of his topological theorem for arbitrary
models.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A concrete residue consequence without exponentiation}

\begin{corollary}[Non-diagonal residue profiles are forced]\label{isg:sc:cor:profiles}
In every nonstandard $M\models\Idelta$, there is an $a\in M$ such that no
ordinary integer has the same residues as $a$ modulo all ordinary positive
integers. Equivalently, $\im\res_{R_M}\not\subseteq\Z$. One can take any
positive infinite $y$ satisfying $\PowDy$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The first conclusion follows from
\cref{isg:sc:prop:diagonal,isg:sc:thm:nochar}. For the explicit version,
induction applied to \cref{isg:sc:lem:successor} yields $\forall
x\,\StdDy(x)$ in $M$. Given a positive infinite $h$, choose a witness
$y\le h<2y$. The witness $y$ is positive infinite and satisfies $\PowDy$.
If its residues matched an integer $m=0$, division by $3$ would contradict
$\PowDy$. If $m\ne0$, divide $y$ by $2^{v_2(|m|)}$; the quotient has odd
remainder modulo $2$, and the same odd-factor construction as in
\cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic} gives a contradiction. This uses only the residue
profile of this element, not a character defined on the entire ring.
\end{proof}

This is an existence theorem for genuinely non-diagonal compatible residue
profiles, not a claim that every profinite integer occurs. Nor does it
assert that exponentiation is total in $\Idelta$. In the language of
\cref{isg:cf:sec:modeltheory}: the factorial type $\tau_*$ realized in a
nonstandard Peano model by $F(h)$ (\cref{isg:cf:thm:PA}) is replaced here
by elements singled out in the \emph{ring} language, by $\PowDy$; they
witness that $\res_{G(M)}(G(M))\ne\Z$ without any recursion inside $M$.

\subsection{Bounded arithmetic is not initially realizable}\label{isg:sc:sec:fragment}
Source~03 never mentions initial realizability. Combined with source~02's
criterion it gives the following, stated and proved at this write.

\begin{definition}[The dyadic fragment; \textup{[write]}]\label{isg:sc:def:Tdy}
Let $\Tdy$ be the theory, in the language $\{0,1,+,\cdot,<\}$, consisting
of $\PAminus$, the sentences
\[
 \ED_n^+:\quad \forall a\ (\exists q\le a)\
 \bigvee_{r=0}^{n-1}a=nq+\underline r\qquad(n=1,2,\ldots),
\]
with $\underline r$ the numeral $1+\cdots+1$, and the single sentence
$\Ind(\StdDy)$ of \eqref{isg:sc:eq:oneind}.
\end{definition}

$\Tdy$ is recursively axiomatized, and $\Idelta\vdash\Tdy$: each
$\ED_n^+$ is proved in the proof of \cref{isg:sc:lem:idiv}, and
$\Ind(\StdDy)$ is an instance of bounded induction, since $\StdDy$ is
$\Delta_0$ and has no parameters. Peano arithmetic therefore proves $\Tdy$
as well. For a model $M$ of $\PAminus$, the sentences $\ED_n^+$ for all
$n$ give standard division in $R_M$, by the last step of the proof of
\cref{isg:sc:lem:idiv}.

\begin{corollary}[Nonstandard bounded arithmetic has no initial additive realization; \textup{[write]}]\label{isg:sc:cor:fragment}
Let $M$ be a nonstandard model of $\Tdy$, for instance a nonstandard model
of $\Idelta$ or of Peano arithmetic, and let $G(M)$ be the ordered abelian
group completion of its additive monoid. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $G(M)$ is a set-sized $\Z$-group with least positive element $1$,
      $\Hom_{\Z}(G(M),\Z)=0$, and $\res_{G(M)}(G(M))\ne\Z$;
\item $G(M)$ is not initially realizable, and there is no additive
      homomorphism $f:G(M)\to\Oz$ with $f(1)=1$, order-preserving or not.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, in \cref{isg:cf:thm:PA} the hypothesis ``Peano arithmetic''
can be replaced by $\Idelta$, and indeed by $\Tdy$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$G(M)$ is the additive group of the difference ring $R_M$, a set because
$M$ is. It is discretely ordered with least positive element $1$, since
$R_M$ is a discretely ordered ring. By the remark after
\cref{isg:sc:def:Tdy}, $R_M$ has standard division, so $G(M)/\Z1$ is
divisible: $a=nq+r$ gives $a+\Z1=n(q+\Z1)$. Thus $G(M)$ is a $\Z$-group.

Suppose $f:G(M)\to\Z$ is a nonzero additive map. By
\cref{isg:sc:thm:characters}, $R_M$ has a normalized integer character
$\chi$. $R_M$ satisfies $\ED_2$ and $\ED_3$, so \cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}
shows that $\StdDy$ defines exactly $\N$ in $M=(R_M)_+$. By
\cref{isg:sc:lem:successor}, which needs only $\ED_2$, the antecedent of
$\Ind(\StdDy)$ holds in $M$, and $M\models\Ind(\StdDy)$ because
$M\models\Tdy$. Hence $M\models\forall x\,\StdDy(x)$, so every element of
$M$ is standard, contradicting the nonstandardness of $M$. Therefore
$\Hom_{\Z}(G(M),\Z)=0$. This is the proof of \cref{isg:sc:thm:nochar}
with $\Idelta$ replaced by the axioms of $\Tdy$ that it uses.

In particular $\Z1$ is not a direct summand of $G(M)$: a projection onto
$\Z1\cong\Z$ would be a nonzero additive map to $\Z$. By
\cref{isg:cf:prop:residuesplit}, $\res_{G(M)}(G(M))\ne\Z$, which proves
(i). By \cref{isg:cf:main:presburger}, a set-sized $\Z$-group whose bottom
cyclic group is not a direct summand is not initially realizable. By
\cref{isg:cf:cor:unit}, it then has no additive homomorphism to $\Oz$
sending $1$ to $1$; alternatively, composing such a map with $\ct$ would be
a nonzero additive map to $\Z$, which is the argument of
\cref{isg:sc:cor:omnific}. This proves (ii). The final sentence follows
from $\mathsf{PA}\vdash\Idelta\vdash\Tdy$.
\end{proof}

At the lower end, open induction does not suffice, even with standard
division.

\begin{proposition}[Open induction is not enough; \textup{[write]}]\label{isg:sc:prop:iopen}
The ring $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ of \cref{isg:sc:sec:shepherdson} is countable
and nonstandard, its nonnegative cone is a model of $\Iopen$ and of every
$\ED_n^+$, and its additive group is an initially realizable $\Z$-group:
\[
 (\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}},+)\ \cong\ V_{\mathrm{Sh}}\lex\Z,\qquad
 V_{\mathrm{Sh}}=\Bigl\{\sum_{i=1}^d a_iX^{q_i}:a_i\in\R_{\rm alg},\
 q_i\in\Q_{>0}\Bigr\}\ \text{divisible}.
\]
Consequently $\Iopen+\{\ED_n^+:n\ge1\}$ does not prove $\Ind(\StdDy)$,
and the conclusion of \cref{isg:sc:cor:fragment} fails for nonstandard
models of $\Iopen$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ is countable because $\R_{\rm alg}$ and $\Q$ are, and
it contains the positive infinite element $X$. Its cone satisfies $\Iopen$
by the imported integer-part theorem recalled in
\cref{isg:sc:sec:shepherdson}, and it has standard division with the
constant term as a ring retraction, as shown there. Every element is
uniquely $m+v$ with $m\in\Z$ and $v\in V_{\mathrm{Sh}}$; dividing the
real algebraic coefficients by an ordinary integer shows that
$V_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ is divisible. A nonzero $v$ has a leading term
$aX^{q}$ with $q>0$, so $m+v$ has the sign of $a$ and $|v|$ exceeds every
ordinary integer. The order is therefore lexicographic with
$V_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ dominant, and $(\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}},+)$ is a $\Z$-group in
which $\Z1$ is a direct summand. \Cref{isg:cf:main:presburger} makes it
initially realizable. If $\Iopen+\{\ED_n^+\}$ proved $\Ind(\StdDy)$, the
cone of $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ would be a nonstandard model of $\Tdy$, and
\cref{isg:sc:cor:fragment} would forbid exactly this splitting.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What this settles in \cref{isg:cf:q:fragment}, and what it does not; \textup{[write]}]\label{isg:sc:rem:fragment}
\Cref{isg:cf:q:fragment} asks for the weakest arithmetic fragment whose
nonstandard models have ordered additive group completions excluded by the
factorial residue type, and for analogous computable residue types giving
sharper bounds. For exclusion from $\IR$ by \emph{any} means,
\cref{isg:sc:cor:fragment,isg:sc:prop:iopen} locate the threshold above
open induction (even with standard division) and at or below
$\Tdy\subseteq\Idelta$, well below the Peano arithmetic of
\cref{isg:cf:thm:PA}. They do not determine the weakest such fragment;
whether $\Tdy$ is strictly weaker than $\Idelta$ is not addressed
(\cref{isg:sc:q:threshold}). The route is not the factorial type: the
factorial function is not provably total in $\Idelta$, and whether every
nonstandard model of $\Idelta$ (or of $\Tdy$) realizes $\tau_*$ in $G(M)$
is not addressed here. In place of $\tau_*$, \cref{isg:sc:cor:profiles}
supplies the elements satisfying $\PowDy$ above the standard cut, whose
residue profiles are non-diagonal. They form an analogous computable
residue obstruction, but one stated in the ring language, not a type in
the ordered-group language of \eqref{isg:cf:eq:type}. The question is
re-scoped accordingly after its statement.
\end{remark}

\section{A uniqueness correction and concrete rings}\label{isg:sc:sec:rings}

\subsection{A correction to a uniqueness lemma}\label{isg:sc:sec:correction}
There is a precise source-level correction relevant to the preceding
character classification. In the terminology of
\cite[Section~2.5]{ELV}, a commutative ring is a \emph{Dorroh ring} when
some ideal has quotient isomorphic to $\Z$; that ideal is said to witness
it. Lemma~2.7 of arXiv:2508.14758v2 asserts that the witnessing ideal is
unique. The claim, as printed on pages~11--12 of that version, is false.

\begin{proposition}[Distinct integer-quotient ideals]\label{isg:sc:prop:correction}
For every $c\in\Z$, the ideal $\mathfrak j_c=(X-c)$ of $\Z[X]$ satisfies
$\Z[X]/\mathfrak j_c\cong\Z$. These ideals are pairwise distinct. This
remains a counterexample when $\Z[X]$ is ordered with $X$ positive infinite
and the extra ordered-Dorroh condition of \cite{ELV} is imposed.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Evaluation at $c$ is a surjective unital ring homomorphism
$\operatorname{ev}_c:\Z[X]\to\Z$. Division by the monic polynomial $X-c$
shows that its kernel is $(X-c)$, proving the quotient statement. If
$c\ne c'$, then $X-c\in\mathfrak j_c$ but
$\operatorname{ev}_{c'}(X-c)=c'-c\ne0$, so $X-c\notin\mathfrak j_{c'}$.

In the leading-coefficient order with $X$ positive infinite, every positive
nonconstant polynomial exceeds every ordinary integer. No $\mathfrak j_c$
contains a nonzero constant, since evaluation fixes constants. Thus every
positive element of each $\mathfrak j_c$ exceeds every integer, which is
exactly the additional ordered condition in question. In particular,
$\mathfrak j_0=(X)$ and $\mathfrak j_1=(X-1)$ already suffice as a
counterexample.
\end{proof}

The error is a confusion between uniqueness of a decomposition \emph{for a
fixed kernel} and independence of the integer coordinate from the kernel.
For the element $X$, the two legitimate decompositions are
\[
 X=X+0\quad\text{relative to }(X),\qquad
 X=(X-1)+1\quad\text{relative to }(X-1).
\]
Agreement of two retractions on the embedded integers does not make their
values on $X$ agree. The following replacement supplies a sufficient
hypothesis directly relevant to omnific arithmetic.

\begin{proposition}[A valid uniqueness replacement]\label{isg:sc:prop:repair}
Let $R$ be a discretely ordered ring satisfying $\ED_2$. There is at most
one normalized additive map $R\to\Z$. Any such map is multiplicative.
Consequently, there is at most one ideal $\mathfrak a$ with
$R/\mathfrak a\cong\Z$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For each ordinary $j$, iterating division by $2$ writes $a=2^jq+r$ with
$r\in\{0,\ldots,2^j-1\}$. Every normalized additive map sends $a$ to an
integer congruent to $r$ modulo $2^j$. Therefore the difference of the
values of two such maps is divisible by all powers of $2$, and is zero.
The same argument applied to $\chi(ab)-\chi(a)\chi(b)$ proves
multiplicativity.

An isomorphism $R/\mathfrak a\cong\Z$ composed with the quotient map
supplies a normalized ring map. It must preserve the identity: a
surjective ring map takes the source identity to the target identity, even
when this is not included in the definition of ring homomorphism. Thus any
two such ideals are kernels of the same map and agree.
\end{proof}

The polynomial ring in \cref{isg:sc:prop:correction} does not satisfy
$\ED_2$: $X=2q+r$ with $q\in\Z[X]$ and $r\in\{0,1\}$ would require a
coefficient $1/2$ in $q$. The added hypothesis therefore excludes the
counterexample for a substantive reason.

This correction is local. The standard-cut argument in
\cite[Theorem~2.9]{ELV} can be run using any \emph{chosen} witnessing
ideal and its retraction; uniqueness is unnecessary for that argument. No
assertion is made that the counterexample invalidates the paper's main
completion theorems. A complete dependency audit of that paper is a
separate task. The correction also does not establish that the error has
not previously been noticed by its authors or by others.

For $\Oz$ the uniqueness is already in the collection: the constant term
is the unique unital ring homomorphism $\Oz\to\Z$
(\replabel{odg:prop:canonicalct}), by the same $2$-adic argument, and this
is the Lean theorem
\fn{Surreal.Foundations.SignSequence.omnific_int_hom_eq_constant} in
\fn{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/Surreal/Foundations/OmnificConstantRigidity.lean}.
\Cref{isg:sc:prop:repair} extends it to normalized \emph{additive} maps and
to every discretely ordered ring with $\ED_2$; that extension is not
formalized. \textup{[}The comparison is the write's.\textup{]}

\subsection{A minimal computable example}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{isg:sc:eq:A0}
 \Asc_0=\Z+X\Q[X]
 =\Bigl\{a_0+\sum_{i=1}^d a_iX^i:\ a_0\in\Z,\ a_i\in\Q\Bigr\},
\end{equation}
with the sign of a nonzero polynomial given by its leading coefficient.
Thus $X$ is positive infinite. Every positive nonconstant polynomial
exceeds all ordinary integers, while positive constants are positive
integers. The ring is discretely ordered, and
\[
 \chi(f)=f(0),\qquad
 f=n\,\frac{f-r}{n}+r,\quad r\equiv f(0)\pmod n,\quad 0\le r<n
\]
give a retraction and standard division. Rational coefficients have exact
finite codes, so equality, order, addition, multiplication, and the
nonnegative domain are computable. All the results above apply.

For clarity, $\Asc_0$ is \emph{not} a model of open induction. Indeed,
$x^2<X$ defines the standard cut on its positive cone, with parameter $X$:
it holds for standard integers, whereas the square of a positive
nonconstant polynomial has degree at least two and exceeds $X$. Its
induction instance fails. The ring is useful here because it isolates the
character and computability phenomena with almost no background theory,
not because it is a particularly strong arithmetic model. The same failed
instance, in the real-coefficient ring $\Z+t\R[t]$ with $t$ positive
infinite, is proved independently by batch-86 manuscript~06, which is
merged into the report
\nolinkurl{docs/foundations-and-computation/polish-models-of-omnific-arithmetic}
(\cref{isg:sec:provenance}).

\begin{example}[Explicit certificates]\label{isg:sc:ex:certificates}
For $y=X$, the forbidden factorization is $y=3(X/3)$. For $y=X+12$, one
has
\[
 y=4\Bigl(2\,\frac{X}{8}+3\Bigr).
\]
For $y=X-12$, whose character is negative, the certificate is
\[
 y=4\Bigl(2\Bigl(\frac{X}{8}-3\Bigr)+3\Bigr).
\]
In each case the displayed witnesses are nonnegative and bounded by $y$,
although some of their integer constant terms are negative. This
illustrates why positivity must be checked in the ordered ring, not after
applying $\chi$.
\end{example}

\subsection{A classical open-induction example}\label{isg:sc:sec:shepherdson}
A more structured example is the finite Puiseux-polynomial ring
\[
 \Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}=\Bigl\{a_0+\sum_{i=1}^d a_iX^{q_i}:\
 a_0\in\Z,\ a_i\in\R_{\rm alg},\ q_i\in\Q_{>0}\Bigr\},
\]
where $\R_{\rm alg}$ denotes the real algebraic numbers and equal
exponents are combined. Order it with $X$ positive infinite. This is the
classical Shepherdson construction \cite{Shepherdson}; the integer-part
characterization of open induction and the Puiseux description are
recalled in \cite{Raffer}.

Here is the structural reason. Put $t=X^{-1}$ and consider the real closed
Puiseux field $K=\bigcup_{n\ge1}\R_{\rm alg}((t^{1/n}))$ with its natural
order. Each series has only finitely many negative exponents. Remove its
positive powers of $t$, retain its finite negative-exponent part, and take
the ordinary integer floor of its algebraic constant. If the constant is an
integer and the discarded infinitesimal tail is negative, subtract one.
The resulting element of $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ is an integer part of the
series. The classical integer-part theorem therefore gives
$(\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}})_+\models\Iopen$. Its use here is an imported
theorem, not a new proof of that characterization.

Constant extraction and division by ordinary integers work exactly as in
$\Asc_0$. Thus $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ fails the single bounded induction
instance \eqref{isg:sc:eq:oneind} while satisfying every quantifier-free
induction instance. Real algebraic numbers admit effective exact
representations, so this ring also has a computable presentation. It
consequently supplies an open-induction example for the complexity theorem
below, and, by \cref{isg:sc:prop:iopen}, an initially realizable one.

\textup{[}Added 4~October 2026, batch~92: with $t=X^{-1}$, the ring
$\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ is also the canonical integer part $\mathcal I^{\Q}$ of
the larger left-finite Levi-Civita field $\mathcal L^{\Q}\supseteq K$ with
coefficients in $\R_{\rm alg}$, in Part~XV of
\nolinkurl{docs/foundations-and-computation/polish-models-of-omnific-arithmetic}
(\replabel{pma:rk:prop:integerpart}; AI-assisted, unrefereed, not
formalized), which is that report's Part~V common integer part with
coefficients in $\R_{\rm alg}$; its floor is the rule described above. Every
field self-embedding of $\mathcal L^{\Q}$ is onto, the exponent group having
rational rank one (\replabel{pma:rk:thm:finite}), so each carries
$\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ to an integer part, and the automorphism
$t\mapsto t+t^2$ carries it to a different one (the paragraph after
\replabel{pma:rk:cor:ipcriterion}). For real coefficients, Part~XIV of that
report shows that the nonnegative cone of
$\Z+\sum_{q\in\Q_{>0}}\R X^q$ satisfies Euclidean division and every
integer-root axiom (\replabel{pma:egt:cor:division},
\replabel{pma:egt:thm:rootspectrum}) but carries no Polish topology with
continuous addition and Borel multiplication
(\replabel{pma:egt:thm:main}); $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ itself is countable.\textup{]}

\subsection{Principal parts of Hahn series}
Let $k$ be an ordered field, $\Gamma$ a nontrivial ordered abelian group,
and use the Hahn convention in which $t^\gamma$ is infinitesimal when
$\gamma>0$. In $k((t^\Gamma))$, define
\[
 \Asc(k,\Gamma)=\Z+\Bigl\{\sum_{\gamma<0}a_\gamma t^\gamma:
       \supp(a)\text{ is well ordered}\Bigr\}.
\]
Every displayed series is a Hahn series with set-sized support. The part
supported on negative exponents is an ideal in this ring: sums of two
negative exponents are negative. Constant extraction is a ring retraction
onto $\Z$, and division of the coefficients by an ordinary integer, with
the usual adjustment of the integer constant term, gives standard
division. A positive nonconstant element has negative leading exponent and
exceeds every ordinary integer. Thus the ring is discretely ordered and
the results above apply.

No claim that this is an integer part of the whole Hahn field is needed.
For example, arbitrary non-Archimedean coefficient fields require
additional care with constants. The discrete ring, its character, and its
division properties are all that the present argument uses.

\subsection{Actual omnific integers}
In Conway normal form, an omnific integer is a surreal number whose
exponents are nonnegative and whose coefficient at exponent zero is an
ordinary integer; its other coefficients are real
(\cref{isg:sec:conventions}). Schematically,
\[
 a=\sum_{i<\alpha}r_i\omega^{a_i},\qquad a_0>a_1>\cdots\ge0,
\]
where the final coefficient, when its exponent is zero, is integral. An
absent zero exponent means zero constant term. The constant-term map
$\ct:\Oz\to\Z$ is additive and multiplicative: with no negative exponents
present, exponent zero in a product arises only from multiplying two
exponent-zero terms.

The positive finite omnific integers are precisely the ordinary positive
integers, so the order is discrete. For an ordinary $n>0$, choose the
ordinary remainder $r$ of $\ct(a)$ modulo $n$; then $(a-r)/n$ again has an
integral constant term and is omnific. Hence $\Oz$ has standard division.
These are the exact ingredients implemented in ProveIt's constant-term and
ordinary-residue development \cite{Residues}
(\cref{isg:sc:sec:relationLean}).

\begin{corollary}[Omnific arithmetic obstruction]\label{isg:sc:cor:omnific}
The formulas $\PowDy$ and $\StdDy$ define respectively the ordinary powers
of two and the standard natural numbers in $\Oz_{\ge0}$. If
$M\models\Idelta$ is nonstandard, there is no additive map $f:M\to\Oz$
satisfying $f(1)=1$. In particular there is no unital semiring embedding
of $M$ into $\Oz_{\ge0}$, and no unital ring embedding of its difference
ring into $\Oz$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The detector applies by the preceding normal-form calculations. Any
proposed additive map extends uniquely to $R_M$ by
$\bar f(a-b)=f(a)-f(b)$. Composing with $\ct$ would be a normalized
integer character, contrary to \cref{isg:sc:thm:nochar}.
\end{proof}

The finite-polynomial map $\Asc_0\hookrightarrow\Oz$ sending $X$ to
$\omega$ is an injective ordered ring homomorphism: distinct polynomial
normal forms remain distinct surreal normal forms. Thus the target contains
natural computable arithmetic-like rings even though it excludes the
additive unit-preserving images of nonstandard bounded arithmetic.
Embedding an ordered field into $\No$ is a different question: its integer
part need not land in $\Oz$. For set-sized $\Z$-groups,
\cref{isg:cf:cor:unit} turns \cref{isg:sc:cor:omnific} into the statement
of \cref{isg:sc:cor:fragment}(ii) about initial realizability.

\subsection{Set-sized workspaces and class language}\label{isg:sc:sec:classes}
All central theorems of source~03 are proved for \emph{set-sized} rings.
Their full omnific applications can be formulated in a class theory such
as NBG, as in \cref{isg:sec:background}, or as a scheme of statements
about definable classes in ZFC. In the latter reading, a formula about
$\Oz$ is an explicitly written first-order formula about set-coded surreal
numbers; one does not postulate a set of all surreals or a truth predicate
for all such formulas.

For the no-map result, the source $M$ is a set. The image of a definable
class map on this set is a set, and composing it with the definable
constant-term operation gives an ordinary set function into $\Z$. The
contradiction is therefore already set-theoretic. The detector's proof is
pointwise and uses only finitely many divisions at each element. For the
semantic and computability theorems that follow, the explicitly set-sized
examples $\Asc_0$ and $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$ suffice. No class satisfaction
construction is hidden in their statements.

\section{Compiling arithmetic into one infinite interval}\label{isg:sc:sec:compiler}
Assume throughout this section the hypotheses of \cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic},
and fix a positive infinite $\Omega\in R$. Although the interval
$[0,\Omega]$ is bounded inside the structure, it contains all ordinary
natural numbers. The standard-cut predicate tells us exactly which elements
to keep.

\subsection{Relativization and correctness}
For an arithmetic formula $\varphi$, define $\varphi^{[\Omega]}$
recursively. Leave atomic formulas unchanged, commute with Boolean
connectives, and set
\begin{align}
 (\exists x\,\psi)^{[\Omega]}&=
    \exists x\le\Omega\;(\StdDy(x)\wedge\psi^{[\Omega]}),\label{isg:sc:eq:etrans}\\
 (\forall x\,\psi)^{[\Omega]}&=
    \forall x\le\Omega\;(\StdDy(x)\to\psi^{[\Omega]}).\label{isg:sc:eq:utrans}
\end{align}
Bound variables in copies of $\StdDy$ are renamed to prevent capture. For
a sentence, the only possibly free variable in the output is $\Omega$.

\begin{lemma}[Truth-preserving relativization]\label{isg:sc:lem:translate}
For every arithmetic formula $\varphi(\bar x)$ and every standard tuple
$\bar a\in\N$, one has
\[
 \N\models\varphi(\bar a)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 R_+\models\varphi^{[\Omega]}(\bar a,\Omega).
\]
The translation is effective and its output is bounded.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Induct on the construction of $\varphi$. Terms with standard inputs take
the same standard values in $\N$ and $R_+$, proving the atomic cases. The
Boolean steps follow immediately. For an existential quantifier, the
elements $x\le\Omega$ satisfying $\StdDy$ are exactly all of $\N$, by
\cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}; apply the induction hypothesis to each such
value. For a universal quantifier the guard discards every nonstandard
value, and the same induction hypothesis applies. Each new quantifier is
bounded, and the fixed formula $\StdDy$ itself uses only bounded
quantifiers. The recursive procedure on finite syntax is an algorithm.
\end{proof}

Before flattening atomic terms, the quantifier nesting depth of the output
is at most $\rk(\varphi)+3$: $\StdDy$ contributes at most three nested
bounded quantifiers, while each original quantifier contributes one. This
estimate is about depth, not a fixed bound on alternations over \emph{all}
input sentences.

\subsection{Literally the same syntactic bound everywhere}
In \eqref{isg:sc:eq:etrans}--\eqref{isg:sc:eq:utrans}, the bounds internal
to $\StdDy$ are variables such as $x$ and $y$. To make every written bound
literally $\Omega$, use
\begin{align}
 \PowDy_\Omega(y):\Longleftrightarrow{}&0<y\ \wedge\
 \forall u\le\Omega\ \forall v\le\Omega\,
 \bigl((u\le y\wedge v\le y)\to y\ne(2u+3)v\bigr),\label{isg:sc:eq:PH}\\
 \StdDy_\Omega(x):\Longleftrightarrow{}&x=0\ \vee\
 \exists y\le\Omega\,
 \bigl(y\le x\wedge x<2y\wedge\PowDy_\Omega(y)\bigr).\label{isg:sc:eq:SH}
\end{align}
For $0\le x\le\Omega$, $\StdDy_\Omega(x)$ and $\StdDy(x)$ are equivalent:
the guards reproduce the smaller original ranges exactly. Replacing
$\StdDy$ by $\StdDy_\Omega$ in the translation therefore preserves the
proof of \cref{isg:sc:lem:translate}.

\subsection{Quadratic atomic normal form}
High-degree input terms do not require high-degree output atoms. For each
local term, introduce a fresh variable for every addition or
multiplication gate. A gate is expressed by an equation such as
\[
 z=x+y\qquad\text{or}\qquad z=xy.
\]
An input atomic comparison is replaced by a bounded existential block
asserting all its gate equations and the final comparison. Every gate
variable is bounded by $\Omega$. For standard inputs, each intermediate
gate value is standard and hence below $\Omega$; the equations also
determine the gate values uniquely. This proves the local equivalence.

The existential blocks must remain \emph{inside the atomic replacement}.
For example, a negated atom becomes the negation of that whole
replacement; one must not move its existential witnesses through negation
without justification. Guards guarantee that the semantic comparison of
source atoms is only required at standard source-variable assignments. The
atoms already present in \eqref{isg:sc:eq:PH}--\eqref{isg:sc:eq:SH} have
total polynomial degree at most two, so no further change is needed there.

\begin{theorem}[Single-bound quadratic compiler]\label{isg:sc:thm:compiler}
There is a computable map $\varphi\mapsto C\varphi(\Omega)$ from
arithmetic sentences to formulas with the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},nosep]
\item every quantifier is bounded by exactly the variable $\Omega$;
\item every atomic equality or inequality has total polynomial degree at
most two in its variables;
\item the only possibly free variable is $\Omega$;
\item for every ring under consideration and every positive infinite
$\Omega$,
\[
 R_+\models C\varphi(\Omega)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\N\models\varphi.
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{isg:sc:lem:translate}, use the guarded equal-bound versions of
the standard-cut formula, and flatten each source atom as described above.
Fresh-variable generation makes every substitution capture-free. The local
gate equivalences and structural induction prove correctness. Each
operation on syntax is effective. Statements (i)--(iii) follow from the
explicit construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Why the infinite bound is indispensable]\label{isg:sc:ex:bound}
The true sentence $\forall x\exists y\,(y=x+1)$ translates to a formula in
which both $x$ and $y$ are bounded by $\Omega$ but $x$ is guarded by
$\StdDy_\Omega(x)$. For every retained $x$, its successor is still
standard and below $\Omega$. The translation is therefore true. If
$\Omega$ were an ordinary finite integer, the retained value $x=\Omega$
would have no allowed successor. A finite-range test cannot substitute for
the infinite-bound proof.
\end{example}

This theorem does not provide a single formula
$\operatorname{Truth}(e,\Omega)$ that takes arbitrary syntax codes as
inputs. Each sentence is compiled into a \emph{different} formula of
potentially greater length and quantifier depth. Consequently it is not a
definable universal truth predicate, and there is no conflict with
Tarski's undefinability theorem.

\subsection{Exact truth complexity in a computable ring}\label{isg:sc:sec:complexity}
Write $\TA$ for the set of codes of first-order sentences true in
$(\N;0,1,+,\cdot,<)$. For sets of natural-number codes, $B\le_mC$ means
that a total computable function maps membership in $B$ exactly to
membership in $C$. Invalid syntax codes are assigned a fixed false sentence
whenever necessary.

Let $T_{\rm one}(\Asc_0,X)$ consist of the true formulas in the fragment
of \cref{isg:sc:thm:compiler}, interpreted in $(\Asc_0)_+$ with
$\Omega=X$. Its atoms have degree at most two, and every quantifier has the
same bound $X$. Let $T_{\Delta_0}(\Asc_0,X)$ denote the entire bounded
truth set in the language with the named element $X$.

\begin{theorem}[True-arithmetic completeness]\label{isg:sc:thm:complexity}
For the computable presentation of $\Asc_0$ in \eqref{isg:sc:eq:A0},
\[
 T_{\rm one}(\Asc_0,X)\equiv_mT_{\Delta_0}(\Asc_0,X)\equiv_m\TA.
\]
More generally, the same conclusion holds for any computably presented
nonstandard discretely ordered ring satisfying the detector hypotheses,
with a specified computable name for a positive infinite element $\Omega$.
It holds, in particular, for $\Asc_{\mathrm{Sh}}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The compiler supplies $\TA\le_mT_{\rm one}$, and inclusion of the
syntactic fragment gives $T_{\rm one}\le_mT_{\Delta_0}$.

For the other direction, a computable presentation has a recursive set of
element codes, recursive operation graphs, and a recursive order relation.
Each of these relations has an effective arithmetical definition: a finite
computation trace of its deciding program can be expressed in ordinary
arithmetic. Replace the atomic relations of a formula by these definitions
and relativize its quantifiers to the presentation's domain. Terms can
first be flattened to operation graphs. Structural induction shows that
the resulting arithmetic sentence is true exactly when the original
sentence is true in the presentation. This is a computable translation of
all first-order truth, hence also of bounded truth, into $\TA$. The
specified name for $\Omega$ is a fixed ordinary code in this translation.
No decision procedure for $\TA$ is used.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Even one outer unbounded quantifier suffices without parameters}
The parameter $\Omega$ can be removed while keeping the matrix bounded.
Let $\operatorname{Inf}(h):=\neg\StdDy(h)$, on the positive cone, and set
\begin{align}
 E_\varphi&:=\exists h\,
        \bigl(\operatorname{Inf}(h)\wedge C\varphi(h)\bigr),\label{isg:sc:eq:Esentence}\\
 U_\varphi&:=\forall h\,
        \bigl(\operatorname{Inf}(h)\to C\varphi(h)\bigr).\label{isg:sc:eq:Usentence}
\end{align}
The first sentence is syntactically $\Sigma_1$ and the second
syntactically $\Pi_1$ in the usual unbounded-quantifier hierarchy over
bounded matrices.

\begin{corollary}[Parameter-free low-level truth]\label{isg:sc:cor:sigma}
For every computable ring in \cref{isg:sc:thm:complexity}, the truth sets
of its parameter-free $\Sigma_1$ sentences and of its parameter-free
$\Pi_1$ sentences are each many-one equivalent to $\TA$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
There is at least one positive infinite $h$, and $\operatorname{Inf}$
defines precisely those elements. By \cref{isg:sc:thm:compiler},
$C\varphi(h)$ has the same truth value for every such $h$, namely the
truth value of $\varphi$ in $\N$. Hence each of
\eqref{isg:sc:eq:Esentence} and \eqref{isg:sc:eq:Usentence} is true
precisely when $\varphi$ is true in $\N$. These give the lower reductions;
the upper reductions are again the computable-presentation translation.
\end{proof}

Syntactic boundedness in a nonstandard computable structure does not mean
that bounded truth is recursive, or even arithmetical as a set of codes.
The interval below an infinite element is externally infinite, and bounded
quantifier alternations over it retain the complexity of arbitrary
arithmetic sentences. The corollary is not a collapse of the ordinary
arithmetical hierarchy in $\N$: it concerns truth in a different
structure.

\subsection{Why Diophantine retractions do not contradict this}\label{isg:sc:sec:diophantine}
For any unital ring retraction $\chi_{\Asc}:\Asc\to\Z$, a finite system of
integer-coefficient polynomial equations has a solution in $\Asc$ if and
only if it has a solution in $\Z$. Apply $\chi_{\Asc}$ to a solution for
the forward direction, and use the constant inclusion for the reverse.
This elementary observation underlies related claims in the repository; it
is not new here. In this collection it is the existence transfer behind
Hilbert's tenth problem over $\Oz$ (\replabel{odg:cor:H10}).

The compiler uses inequalities, negations, and alternating bounded
quantifiers. Retractions need not preserve order, as $X-1$ illustrates,
and do not transport these quantifiers. Therefore the equality-only
existential observation places no upper bound on the bounded truth sets
just studied. In particular, it must not be confused with the stronger
notion of Diophantine correctness for formulas allowing inequalities.

\section{Standard systems and computable scalar fields}\label{isg:ptg:sec:standard}

\subsection{The fourth source}\label{isg:ptg:sec:overview}
\Cref{isg:ptg:sec:standard,isg:ptg:sec:perfect,isg:ptg:sec:residues,isg:ptg:sec:hahn}
are source~04, \emph{Perfect Transcendence Gaps in Borel Arithmetic:
Standard Systems, $p$-adic Shadows, Hahn Fields, and Omnific Integers}
(26 pages, 3~October 2026). It does not mention initial subgroups or this
report. It was placed here because its exact profinite image
(\cref{isg:ptg:thm:profinite}) answers \cref{isg:sc:q:images}, a question
this report names, for nonstandard models of Peano arithmetic; the
question asks about $\Idelta$, and is re-scoped after its statement.
Everything in these four sections is source~04's, with the symbols renamed
as in \cref{isg:ptg:sec:conventions}, except the corollary marked
\textup{[write~04]} and the bracketed comparisons, each of which says that
it was added at this write.

\paragraph{The questions, as source~04 states them.} The overlap is between
two concrete themes. Glazer studies descriptive restrictions on models of
arithmetic, including the failure of Borel models to have full standard
system and automatic-continuity obstructions to topological arithmetic
\cite{GlazerSS,Glazer}. ProveIt contains formal and exploratory work on
arithmetic coding, computability, Conway normal forms, omnific integers and
ordinary congruence completions \cite{ProveItPTG}. Source~04 asks what a
standard-system restriction says about the \emph{coefficient fields} used
in the latter constructions. Does failure to code every real merely omit
some names, or does it leave an irreparable algebraic deficit? Can
adjoining countably many external constants repair the deficit? Can
adding arbitrary well-ordered Hahn supports repair it? Can finite
congruence data detect what is missing? The first two questions are
answered negatively, and the text explains exactly why the third can also
have a negative answer.

\paragraph{A general theorem behind the arithmetic.} Throughout
\cref{isg:ptg:sec:standard,isg:ptg:sec:perfect,isg:ptg:sec:residues,isg:ptg:sec:hahn},
``analytic'' refers to a continuous image of a Polish space, or
equivalently to a projection of a Borel set. It does not refer to
power-series analyticity. The work is in ordinary ZFC unless a different
foundation is explicitly discussed.

\begin{theorem}[Perfect gap after countably many parameters]\label{isg:ptg:thm:headline}
Let $F$ be $\R$ or $\Qp$, and let $k\subsetneq F$ be an analytic subfield
relatively algebraically closed in $F$. For any countable $Y\subset F$, put
\[
 k_Y=\acl_F(k(Y))
 =\{x\in F:x\text{ is algebraic over }k(Y)\}.
\]
Then $k_Y$ is a proper analytic dense subfield of $F$, is null for additive
Haar measure, and is meager. For each $n\geq1$, the set of algebraically
dependent $n$-tuples over $k_Y$ is analytic, null, and meager in $F^n$.
Every nonempty open $U\subset F$ therefore contains a Cantor set
algebraically independent over $k_Y$, and
\[
 \trdeg(F/k_Y)=\cfrak.
\]
\end{theorem}

The word \emph{countable} concerns an external set of parameters. There is
no hypothesis that those parameters are computable or belong to a model.
The theorem also permits $k$ to be uncountable. Consequently a proof that
simply enumerates all polynomials over $k$ would be invalid. The aggregate
dependence relations, rather than the individual polynomials, are the
objects to which category and measure arguments are applied. The proof is
assembled in \cref{isg:ptg:sec:perfectsets}.

\paragraph{The arithmetic and omnific conclusions.} For a Turing ideal
$\mathcal S$, the field $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)$ is defined in
\cref{isg:ptg:sec:fields}. Write $\mathcal K_\infty(\mathcal S)=\mathcal K_\R(\mathcal S)$
and $\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)=\mathcal K_{\Qp}(\mathcal S)$.

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic-to-Hahn gap]\label{isg:ptg:thm:arithmetic}
Let $M$ be a nonstandard Borel model of $\PA$ and $\mathcal S=\SSy(M)$.
For each $F=\R,\Qp$, the field $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)$ satisfies the
hypotheses, hence the conclusions, of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline}.
Moreover:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\arabic*)}]
\item For the difference ring $R_M$, the residue homomorphism
$\res_p:R_M\to\Zp$ has image $\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)\cap\Zp$. Its image
is dense, null, and meager, and its fraction field is
$\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$.
\item If $Y\subset\R$ is countable and
$k_{\R,Y}=\acl_\R(\mathcal K_\infty(\mathcal S)(Y))$, there is a Cantor set
$P\subset(1,2)$ whose elements are algebraically independent even over the
Hahn field $k_{\R,Y}((t^\Q))$ inside $\R((t^\Q))$.
\item Under $t=\omega^{-1}$, the family $\{r\omega:r\in P\}$ consists of
purely infinite omnific integers, is algebraically independent over
$k_{\R,Y}((t^\Q))$, and maps to zero modulo every positive ordinary
integer.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\textup{[}Its parts are proved as
\cref{isg:ptg:thm:scalar,isg:ptg:cor:advice,isg:ptg:thm:residue,isg:ptg:cor:residuesmall,isg:ptg:cor:hahnarith,isg:ptg:thm:omnific};
this pointer is the write's.\textup{]} In the last assertion the \emph{coefficients} form a Cantor set in the real
topology. The resulting omnific family is not asserted to be perfect in the
surreal order topology; indeed, it is discrete in that topology. This
distinction is proved in \cref{isg:ptg:sec:topologies}. A complementary
reconstruction theorem identifies the full profinite image and recovers
$\mathcal S$ as its Boolean algebra of idempotents
(\cref{isg:ptg:thm:profinite,isg:ptg:cor:idempotents}).

\paragraph{Relation to previous work and novelty boundary, as source~04
states it.} Fatalini and Schindler explicitly credit independent
observations of Ben de Bondt and Glazer that adding any new real to a
suitable set-theoretic universe yields maximal transcendence degree over
its old real field \cite[Section~2]{FataliniSchindler}. This is a close
conceptual precursor, not a new result of source~04. Their setting involves
reals of nested universes; source~04 uses external analytic fields attached
to one arithmetic standard system and obtains perfect, measure, category,
$p$-adic, and Hahn conclusions. Ye, Yu, and Zhao study analytic subfields
of both $\R$ and $\Qp$, including obstructions to maximal subfields
omitting a specified point \cite[Proposition~2.6]{YeYuZhao}. Their paper
appeared online in 2026, so it is included in the novelty check rather than
overlooked. Edgar and Miller's dimension theorem gives a further
consequence for the real fields: their Hausdorff dimension is zero
\cite{EdgarMiller}. The existence of perfect independent sets once the
relevant relations are meager is Mycielski's classical theorem
\cite{Mycielski,DolezalKubis}.

The results developed in source~04 are the countable-parameter derivation
argument, the assembled perfect-gap theorem, the exact
standard-system/residue identifications (including profinite idempotents),
and their coefficient-preserving Hahn and congruence applications. Some
individual ingredients are elementary or may already occur in the
literature in another form. The search undertaken for source~04 does not
establish first publication. The mathematical claims stand on their stated
proofs and dependencies, not on a ``breakthrough'' label. In particular,
neither the general Scott-set problem nor the reverse-mathematical questions
in Glazer's topological paper are claimed resolved.

\paragraph{Relation to this report and its neighbours.}
\textup{[}This paragraph is the write's; source~04 cites neither this report
nor the other reports named here.\textup{]} The difference ring $R_M$ is
that of \cref{isg:sc:sec:nogo} (source~04 writes $D=M-M$), and $\res_p$,
$\res_{R_M}$ are the coordinates and the whole of the canonical map
$\res_R$ of \cref{isg:sc:sec:residues}. \Cref{isg:ptg:thm:profinite}
strengthens \cref{isg:sc:cor:profiles}, which says only that
$\im\res_{R_M}\not\subseteq\Z$, to an exact description in the Peano case,
and so answers \cref{isg:sc:q:images} for Peano arithmetic
(\cref{isg:ptg:sec:images}). \Cref{isg:ptg:cor:nomap}, no unital ring map
$R_M\to\Z$, is weaker than \cref{isg:sc:thm:nochar,isg:sc:cor:noadd,isg:sc:cor:omnific},
which exclude every additive map sending $1$ to $1$, already for $\Idelta$.
The ring $\Asc(k,\Q)$ of \cref{isg:ptg:sec:comparison} is source~03's
$\Asc(k,\Gamma)$ at $\Gamma=\Q$; \cref{isg:ptg:prop:tail} and the
$\Asc$-half of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:completions} are its set-sized versions of
facts proved in Lean for the class-sized $\Oz$, and the point of
\cref{isg:ptg:thm:completions} is the one made after
\cref{isg:sc:prop:diagonal}: the canonical image, not the completion,
separates the rings. \Cref{isg:ptg:thm:hahn} is an immediate consequence of
a lemma of the independent-surreal-copies report
(\replabel{isc:cp:lem:coeffld}). These relations are recorded at the
statements concerned and in \cref{isg:sec:provenance}.

\subsection{Conventions of the fourth source}\label{isg:ptg:sec:conventions}
Source~04 works in ZFC, with set-sized Hahn fields only
(\cref{isg:ptg:sec:classes}). Several of its symbols are renamed here,
because the letters are in use elsewhere in this report. The renaming
changes no statement.

$P$ for an independent Cantor set; $t$ for the Hahn variable, with
$t^\gamma$ infinitesimal for $\gamma>0$ as in \cref{isg:sc:sec:rings};
$\ct$ for constant extraction (\cref{isg:sec:conventions}). Letters used
only inside one proof ($U$, $V$ for neighbourhoods, $m(X)$ for a minimal
polynomial, $a$, $c$ for elements of $M$, $T_j$ for indeterminates) are
local to that proof. In \cref{isg:ptg:rem:analytic} source~04's $t$ is
written $\tau$, since $t$ is the Hahn variable, and where source~04 writes
$Y$ for a polynomial variable it is $X_n$ or $X_2$ here, since $Y$ is the
parameter set. The shipped records of source~04 use its own letters
and its own numbering. Its statements are numbered here as follows.
