\subsection{Borel models and external traces}\label{isg:ptg:sec:traces}
A Borel model, in \cref{isg:ptg:sec:standard,isg:ptg:sec:perfect,isg:ptg:sec:residues,isg:ptg:sec:hahn},
has a standard Borel underlying set, with Borel addition and
multiplication, and satisfies $\PA$ in the usual first-order sense. One may
choose a compatible Polish presentation of the underlying standard Borel
space. The topology is presentation data, not an order topology that is
assumed to make arithmetic continuous.

A standard numeral is the interpretation of an ordinary $n\in\N$. An element
is nonstandard when it exceeds every standard numeral. A model is
nonstandard when it has such an element. The set of standard numerals is an
external subset; it is not assumed internally definable.

For $a\in M$, define its standard binary trace
\[
 b_M(a)=\{n\in\N:M\models\Bit(a,\bar n)=1\}.
\]
The standard system is
\[
 \mathcal S=\SSy(M)=\{b_M(a):a\in M\}\subseteq2^\N.
\]
Here bits are least-significant first, including zeros beyond the ordinary
length for standard integers. This convention is useful for residues.

\begin{lemma}[Coding definable traces]\label{isg:ptg:lem:traces}
In a nonstandard model of $\PA$, every set
$\{n\in\N:M\models\varphi(\bar n,\bar a)\}$ belongs to $\SSy(M)$.
Consequently the binary-trace definition agrees with the definition using
all parameter-definable subsets of the standard cut.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For every arithmetic formula $\varphi(i,\bar a)$, full induction proves
finite binary comprehension:
\[
 \forall b\ \exists c\ \forall i<b\,
 [\Bit(c,i)=1\ \longleftrightarrow\ \varphi(i,\bar a)].
\]
One proves this by induction on $b$, choosing whether to add $2^b$ at the
successor step and maintaining $c<2^b$. Classical excluded middle is used
for the instance of $\varphi$; full induction allows its complexity.
Evaluate the theorem at any nonstandard $b\in M$. The resulting $c$ has
the required bits at every standard position. The converse is immediate
because the bit relation is arithmetic.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Turing-ideal closure]\label{isg:ptg:lem:ideal}
$\mathcal S=\SSy(M)$ is a nonempty Turing ideal: it contains every
computable set, is closed under finite Turing joins, and is downward closed
under Turing reducibility.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The even/odd interleaving of two traces is a definable trace, so finite
joins are covered by \cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces}. Suppose an oracle program
computes the characteristic function of $\mathsf B$ from
$\mathsf A=b_M(a)$, and is total on the standard inputs. In $M$, use the bit
oracle of $a$ and define $\varphi(n,a)$ to mean that the deterministic
oracle computation halts with output one.

For a standard input, the actual computation is a standard finite run and
uses only standard positions of $\mathsf A$. That same run exists in $M$.
Arithmetic proves determinism and the impossibility of two incompatible
halting outputs. Thus a nonstandard purported run cannot change the answer
already furnished by the standard halting run. The standard trace of
$\varphi$ is precisely $\mathsf B$, and \cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces} applies.
Taking an oracle-free program proves that all computable sets occur.
Nonemptiness is also witnessed by the empty set.
\end{proof}

This argument does not need the stronger Scott-set path property. The
Turing-ideal part is exactly what the subsequent computable-field
construction consumes. Conversely, no claim is made that an arbitrary
Turing ideal is the standard system of a model of arithmetic.

\subsection{Analyticity and the external input from Glazer}\label{isg:ptg:sec:glazer}
\begin{lemma}[The trace image is analytic]\label{isg:ptg:lem:analyticss}
If $M$ is Borel, $b_M:M\to2^\N$ is Borel, and $\SSy(M)$ is analytic.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each fixed positive standard $m$, $\PA$ proves the existence and
uniqueness of a remainder $r<m$ in division by $m$. For each standard
$r<m$, the statement $a=mq+r$ for some $q\in M$ defines an analytic set of
$a$, since arithmetic is Borel. Its complement is the finite union of the
corresponding analytic sets for the other remainders. It is therefore
Borel by the analytic/coanalytic separation theorem. The $n$th standard
bit is determined by the remainder modulo $2^{n+1}$, so each coordinate of
$b_M$ is Borel. Its image is analytic.
\end{proof}

Source~04 uses the following external result in precisely the indicated
strength.

\begin{fact}[Glazer's standard-system theorem; input]\label{isg:ptg:fact:glazer}
A Borel model of elementary function arithmetic does not have full
standard system. In particular, for a nonstandard Borel model $M$ of
$\PA$, one has $\SSy(M)\ne2^\N$.
\end{fact}

Glazer states the stronger Borel-quotient version in his February 2024
seminar slides \cite[slide~4]{GlazerSS}. Source~04 neither reproves that
result nor claims its weaker metatheory for the new arguments below. Its
applications use ordinary Borel models of $\PA$ in ZFC. The distinction is
substantive: the full induction used in \cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces} should
not silently be replaced by bounded induction.
\textup{[}Added at this write: source~04 cites the theorem from the
seminar announcement and slides; this write consulted neither and did not
look for a published proof. Every statement below about a Borel model
depends on it, through the properness of $\SSy(M)$. Statements that do not
mention Borel models do not depend on it; among them are the exact images
of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:residue,isg:ptg:thm:profinite}, the Boolean
reconstruction of \cref{isg:ptg:cor:idempotents}, and
\cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces,isg:ptg:lem:ideal,isg:ptg:prop:faithful,isg:ptg:lem:roots,isg:ptg:cor:nomap,isg:ptg:cor:images,isg:ptg:thm:hahn}.\textup{]}

\begin{corollary}[A preliminary size gap]\label{isg:ptg:cor:sssmall}
If $M$ is a nonstandard Borel model of $\PA$, $\SSy(M)$ is a dense null
meager subset of Cantor space.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Under symmetric difference it is a subgroup, by computability closure. It
contains the finite sets, so is dense. It is analytic and proper. An
analytic subgroup has the Baire property and is Haar measurable. A
nonmeager or positive-measure subgroup is open by the category or measure
difference-set theorem. An open subgroup is closed, and a dense closed
subgroup is the whole group. This contradicts properness.
\end{proof}

Nullness alone does not yet prove the desired algebraic independence. The
missing work is to preserve properness after adjoining parameters and
taking roots, and then to control dependence in every finite arity.

\subsection{Fields computable from a Turing ideal}\label{isg:ptg:sec:fields}
Use the usual rational presentations of $\R$ and $\Qp$. A fast name for
$x\in\R$ is a rational sequence $(q_n)$ with $|q_n-x|\leq2^{-n}$. For
$x\in\Qp$, use $|q_n-x|_p\leq p^{-n}$. A name is computable from
$\mathsf A\subseteq\N$ when an oracle program with oracle $\mathsf A$
outputs the rational $q_n$ on input $n$.

\begin{definition}[Computable scalar field]\label{isg:ptg:def:field}
For a nonempty Turing ideal $\mathcal S$ and $F\in\{\R,\Qp\}$, set
\[
 \mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)=\{x\in F:\text{some fast name of }x
                   \text{ is computable from some }\mathsf A\in\mathcal S\}.
\]
No uniform choice of a name or an oracle for every element is part of the
definition.
\end{definition}

Rational field operations are computable on fast names. To invert a
nonzero point, one searches for a certified positive lower bound on its
absolute value; the search terminates because the point is nonzero. Finite
joins combine the coefficient oracles. Hence $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)$ is
a subfield containing $\Q$, and it is dense in $F$.

\begin{lemma}[Analyticity of the scalar field]\label{isg:ptg:lem:analyticfield}
If $\mathcal S\subseteq2^\N$ is analytic, then
$\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)\subseteq F$ is analytic.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed oracle-program index $e$, the relation ``the $e$th program with
oracle $\mathsf A$ outputs a total rational sequence $(q_n)$, and
$|q_n-x|_F\leq\epsilon_n$ for all $n$'' is Borel in $(\mathsf A,x)$.
Halting at each input is a countable Boolean combination of finite-oracle
conditions, and each approximation inequality is closed in $x$ once the
finite computation is fixed. Intersect with $\mathsf A\in\mathcal S$,
project to $x$, and take the countable union over $e$. Analytic sets are
closed under these operations.
\end{proof}

\subsection{No information is lost by passing to a local field}
For $\mathsf A\in2^\N$, consider the two encodings
\begin{align}
 c_\infty(\mathsf A)&=\sum_{n\geq0}2\mathsf A(n)3^{-(n+1)},\label{isg:ptg:eq:cantorcode}\\
 c_p(\mathsf A)&=\sum_{n\geq0}\mathsf A(n)p^n.\label{isg:ptg:eq:pcode}
\end{align}
The first lies in the ordinary ternary Cantor set, and the second lies in
$\Zp$. Both have a fast name computable from $\mathsf A$. Conversely, a fast
name recovers the digits, uniformly on the indicated image. In the real
case the two child intervals are separated by a nonempty middle gap. In the
$p$-adic case a sufficiently accurate approximation determines the next
residue, and hence the next digit. There is no binary-expansion ambiguity
in either construction.

\begin{proposition}[Faithful passage from ideals to fields]\label{isg:ptg:prop:faithful}
For Turing ideals $\mathcal S,\mathcal T$ and $F\in\{\R,\Qp\}$,
\[
 \mathcal S\subseteq\mathcal T\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)\subseteq\mathcal K_F(\mathcal T).
\]
In particular, $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)=F$ if and only if
$\mathcal S=2^\N$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The forward implication is immediate. For the reverse, take
$\mathsf A\in\mathcal S$. Then $c_F(\mathsf A)\in\mathcal K_F(\mathcal T)$,
so it has a fast name computable from some $\mathsf B\in\mathcal T$. Digit
recovery gives $\mathsf A\leq_T\mathsf B$, hence $\mathsf A\in\mathcal T$.
For the final assertion, every point of $F$ has some fast name, and the
graph of a name can itself be coded by a subset of $\N$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Relative algebraic closure: the effective root argument}
\begin{lemma}[Individual roots are oracle-computable]\label{isg:ptg:lem:roots}
Let $f\in F[X]$ be nonzero, with its finitely many coefficients computable
from one oracle $\mathsf A$. Each root $\alpha\in F$ of $f$ has a fast name
computable from $\mathsf A$. The assertion is nonuniform in the choice of
the root and its finite isolating data.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $\alpha$ has multiplicity $m$, replace $f$ by $f^{(m-1)}$. Its
coefficients remain $\mathsf A$-computable, and $\alpha$ is now a simple
root. The integer $m$ is a finite parameter; no algorithm to find all
multiplicities uniformly is being asserted.

For $F=\R$, a rational root already has a computable name. For an
irrational root, choose rational endpoints surrounding $\alpha$ in a small
interval on which $f'$ has constant nonzero sign and $f$ has opposite signs
at the endpoints. The endpoints are finite data. Bisection computes
$\alpha$: at any rational midpoint the value of $f$ is nonzero, because
$\alpha$ is the only root in the interval and is irrational. Its sign is
therefore eventually certified by refining the coefficient approximations.
The nested interval lengths provide a computable error bound.

For $F=\Qp$, make a rational scaling of the variable and multiply the
polynomial by a power of $p$ so that the chosen root and all coefficients
are integral. These are again finite choices; they can be undone at the
end. Write the resulting polynomial as $g$. Choose a rational $a\in\Zp$
sufficiently close to the simple root that
\[
 s=v_p(g'(a))\geq0,\qquad
 \delta=v_p(g(a))-2s>0.
\]
A rational root is handled separately. For a nonrational root such an $a$
exists by continuity and simplicity. Newton iteration
\[
 a_0=a,\qquad a_{j+1}=a_j-\frac{g(a_j)}{g'(a_j)}
\]
is computable from $\mathsf A$. Taylor expansion over $\Zp$ shows
inductively that the derivative valuation stays $s$, while
\[
 v_p(g(a_j))\geq 2s+2^j\delta,
 \qquad v_p(a_j-\alpha)\geq s+2^j\delta.
\]
For the induction step, the linear term is canceled by Newton's correction;
the remaining terms have at least twice the valuation of that correction.
Its valuation exceeds $s$, so the derivative valuation does not change. The
same estimates show that the limit is the unique root in the selected
Hensel neighbourhood. The displayed error estimate turns the sequence into
a fast name. All divisions are by nonzero quantities with the fixed
certified valuation. No undecidable test of whether an intermediate
residual is exactly zero is needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Relatively algebraically closed scalar fields]\label{isg:ptg:thm:scalar}
For every Turing ideal $\mathcal S$, $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)$ is
relatively algebraically closed in $F$. If $\mathcal S$ is proper and
analytic, it is a proper analytic relatively algebraically closed dense
subfield. Furthermore,
\[
 \mathcal K_\infty(\mathcal S)\preccurlyeq (\R,+,\cdot,<),\qquad
 \mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)\preccurlyeq(\Qp,+,\cdot)
\]
in their respective field languages.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $\alpha\in F$ is algebraic over $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)$, combine
oracles for the finitely many coefficients of an annihilating polynomial.
Their join belongs to $\mathcal S$, and \cref{isg:ptg:lem:roots} gives a
name for $\alpha$. Analyticity and properness follow from
\cref{isg:ptg:lem:analyticfield,isg:ptg:prop:faithful}.

A relatively algebraically closed subfield of $\R$ is real closed: positive
square roots and roots of odd-degree polynomials lie in $\R$ and are
algebraic. Quantifier elimination for real closed fields then gives the
elementary embedding.

For the $p$-adic assertion use Macintyre's quantifier elimination in the
language with power predicates \cite{Macintyre}. A unary formula over
$\mathcal K=\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ is a Boolean combination of
polynomial equalities and conditions that polynomial values are $n$th
powers. If a witnessing point annuls a nonzero polynomial over
$\mathcal K$, it belongs to $\mathcal K$ by relative algebraic closure.
Otherwise all tested nonzero polynomial values stay nonzero locally, and
their power classes are locally constant: $(\Qp^\times)^n$ is open by
Hensel's lemma. Thus the same formula holds on a neighbourhood containing a
rational point. This proves the Tarski--Vaught condition. Power predicates
agree on $\mathcal K$ and $\Qp$ by relative algebraic closure, so the claim
also holds in the pure ring language.
\end{proof}

The elementary-subfield assertion is logically distinct from computability.
It says that the small field satisfies the same first-order field theory
with its own parameters. It does not make it topologically complete and
does not provide a computable selector for solutions of arbitrary formulas.

\section{The analytic-derivation obstruction and perfect gaps}\label{isg:ptg:sec:perfect}

\subsection{No positive countable transcendence degree}\label{isg:ptg:sec:derivation}
The following lemma is the main mechanism for surviving external
parameters. The zero-transcendence-degree case is intentionally excluded
in its statement; relative algebraic closure handles that case in the
application.

\begin{lemma}[No positive countable transcendence degree]\label{isg:ptg:lem:derivation}
Let $F=\R$ or $\Qp$, and let $k\subseteq F$ be an analytic subfield. Then
$\trdeg(F/k)$ is not a positive finite or countably infinite cardinal.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $\mathbf b=(b_j)_{j\in I}$ is a nonempty finite or countable
transcendence basis, with $0\in I\subseteq\N$. On the rational function
field $k(\mathbf b)$ define a $k$-derivation $\partial$ by
\[
 \partial b_0=1,\qquad \partial b_j=0\quad(j\ne0).
\]
Algebraic independence makes formal differentiation well defined. Because
the characteristic is zero, the algebraic extension $F/k(\mathbf b)$ is
separable, and $\partial$ has a unique extension $\widetilde\partial:F\to F$.
For completeness, if $m(X)$ is a separable minimal polynomial of $\alpha$,
the only possible extension is
\[
 \widetilde\partial(\alpha)=-\frac{m^{\partial}(\alpha)}{m'(\alpha)},
\]
where $m^{\partial}$ differentiates the coefficients. This formula defines a
compatible derivation on each simple finite extension; uniqueness makes
the definitions agree on overlaps and hence on their union.

We now prove that $\widetilde\partial$ is Borel. A pair $(x,y)\in F^2$
belongs to its graph if and only if there is a polynomial $Q$ with
coefficients in $k$, in a finite list of the indeterminates
$(T_j)_{j\in I}$ and one more indeterminate $X$, such that
\begin{align}
 Q(\mathbf b,x)&=0,\label{isg:ptg:eq:witness1}\\
 Q_X(\mathbf b,x)&\ne0,\label{isg:ptg:eq:witness2}\\
 yQ_X(\mathbf b,x)+Q_{T_0}(\mathbf b,x)&=0.\label{isg:ptg:eq:witness3}
\end{align}
Here $Q_{T_0}=0$ when $T_0$ does not occur. Every $x$ has such a witness:
clear denominators in its separable minimal polynomial over $k(\mathbf b)$.
The clearing factor does not vanish at $\mathbf b$, so condition
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:witness2} survives. Differentiating
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:witness1} proves necessity of
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:witness3}; it also proves that any witness yields the
unique value $\widetilde\partial(x)$.

There are countably many choices of finite variable lists and monomial
patterns. For each pattern, the coefficient tuples form a finite power of
the analytic set $k$, and the displayed constraints are Borel conditions
given by continuous polynomial evaluation. Projection and a countable union
show that the graph is analytic. No enumeration of the elements of $k$ has
been used.

A total single-valued function between Polish spaces with analytic graph
is Borel: for any open target set $V$, both its inverse image and its
complement are analytic, obtained by projecting the graph restricted to $V$
and to its complement. They are therefore Borel. Since
$\widetilde\partial$ is additive, the automatic-continuity theorem for
Borel homomorphisms of Polish groups makes it continuous. Finally
$\widetilde\partial$ vanishes on $\Q$, and $\Q$ is dense in both possible
fields $F$. Hence $\widetilde\partial=0$, contradicting
$\widetilde\partial(b_0)=1$.
\end{proof}

Here is the small category argument underlying automatic continuity, to
expose its exact role. Given a target neighbourhood $U$ of zero, choose a
symmetric neighbourhood $V$ whose fourfold sum is contained in $U$. Cover
the separable target by countably many translates of $V$. One of their
inverse images is nonmeager and has the Baire property. The difference of
that inverse image with itself contains a neighbourhood of zero by
Pettis's theorem, and the image of this difference lies in $V-V\subseteq
U$. This is continuity at zero. The Baire-property and analytic-graph facts
used here are standard descriptive set theory \cite{Kechris}.

\begin{proposition}[Countable algebraic hulls remain proper]\label{isg:ptg:prop:hulls}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline}, for every countable
$Y\subset F$ the field $k_Y=\acl_F(k(Y))$ is analytic, relatively
algebraically closed in $F$, and proper.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The field $k(Y)$ is the union of values of rational expressions with
finitely many coefficients from $k$ and finitely many parameters from $Y$,
restricted to nonzero denominators. The family of expression patterns and
parameter lists is countable. Each range is analytic. The set of roots in
$F$ of nonzero polynomials over this generated field is likewise analytic.
It is a subfield, and transitivity of algebraic extensions proves relative
algebraic closure.

If $k_Y=F$, then $\trdeg(F/k)\leq\aleph_0$. It cannot be zero, because $k$
is relatively algebraically closed in $F$ and proper. It cannot be positive
and countable by \cref{isg:ptg:lem:derivation}. This contradiction proves
properness.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why the analytic hypothesis matters]\label{isg:ptg:rem:analytic}
With unrestricted choice one can choose a transcendence basis $T$ of
$F/\Q$, remove one member $\tau$, and form
$k=\acl_F(\Q(T\setminus\{\tau\}))$. This is a proper relatively
algebraically closed subfield with $\trdeg(F/k)=1$ and
$\acl_F(k(\tau))=F$. The lemma proves that this $k$ cannot be analytic.
Thus the hypothesis excludes an actual failure mode, not merely an
inconvenient proof technique.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Nullness and meagerness of every hull}
\begin{lemma}[Proper analytic subfields are small]\label{isg:ptg:lem:small}
Every proper analytic subfield of $\R$ or $\Qp$ is dense, null, and meager.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
It contains $\Q$, hence is dense. It has the Baire property and is
measurable for the completed additive Haar measure. A positive-measure set
has a difference set containing a neighbourhood of zero; a nonmeager set
with the Baire property has the same conclusion. For a subgroup the
difference set is the subgroup itself. It would therefore be open, hence
also closed, and density would force equality with the ambient field. This
contradicts properness.
\end{proof}

For $\Qp$, density is important: a proper open additive subgroup such as
$\Zp$ certainly exists. It is the field's containment of the dense prime
field, not an assertion that $\Qp$ is connected, that rules out this
possibility.

\subsection{Aggregate polynomial dependence}
For a subfield $k'\subseteq F$, define
\[
 \Dep_n(k')=\{\bar x\in F^n:
   \exists Q\in k'[X_1,\ldots,X_n]\setminus\{0\},\ Q(\bar x)=0\}.
\]
Repeated coordinates are dependent, but the definition also makes sense
for arbitrary ordered tuples. If $k'$ is analytic, this set is analytic:
use countably many finite monomial patterns and project over the nonzero
coefficient tuples in finite powers of $k'$.

\begin{proposition}[All dependence relations are null and meager]\label{isg:ptg:prop:relations}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline}, $\Dep_n(k_Y)$ is null
and meager in $F^n$ for every $n\geq1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The case $n=1$ is $\Dep_1(k_Y)=k_Y$, because $k_Y$ is relatively
algebraically closed. It is null and meager by
\cref{isg:ptg:prop:hulls,isg:ptg:lem:small}.

Suppose the claim holds for $n-1$. Fix an $(n-1)$-tuple $\bar a$
algebraically independent over $k_Y$. In a nonzero polynomial
$Q(\bar X,X_n)$ over $k_Y$, at least one coefficient as a polynomial in
$X_n$ is a nonzero polynomial in $\bar X$. Independence ensures it does not
vanish at $\bar a$. Thus
\[
 \{x:(\bar a,x)\in\Dep_n(k_Y)\}
      =\acl_F(k_Y(\bar a))
      =\acl_F(k(Y\cup\{a_1,\ldots,a_{n-1}\})).
\]
The last equality follows by algebraic transitivity in both directions. It
is a proper null meager field by the preceding propositions. For dependent
$\bar a$, the section is the whole of $F$, but those base points form a
null meager set by induction.

The full relation is analytic, hence universally measurable and has the
Baire property. Fubini's theorem, on finite-measure boxes and then on a
countable cover of $F^n$, proves nullness. The Kuratowski--Ulam theorem
proves meagerness. These arguments apply to completed measures; no
Borelness stronger than analyticity is required.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Perfect sets and cardinality}\label{isg:ptg:sec:perfectsets}
Mycielski's theorem says that in a perfect completely metrizable space, a
countable family of meager relations of finite arities has a perfect
independent set; one can take a compact Cantor subset
\cite{Mycielski,DolezalKubis}. Apply it in any nonempty open $U\subset F$
to the restrictions of the relations $\Dep_n(k_Y)$. Open subspaces of a
Polish space are Polish, and these spaces have no isolated points. The
resulting Cantor set $P$ has no polynomial dependence among any finite
tuple of distinct members. Consequently $|P|=\cfrak$, so
$\trdeg(F/k_Y)\geq\cfrak$; the reverse inequality is $|F|=\cfrak$. This
completes the proof of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline}.

There are two different ``largeness'' assertions here. The set of all
independent $n$-tuples is conull and comeager, whereas the particular
Cantor set furnished by the theorem may have measure zero. No
positive-measure independent Cantor set is claimed. For any probability law
on $F^n$ absolutely continuous with respect to Haar measure, an $n$-tuple
sampled from that law is independent over $k_Y$ with probability one.

\begin{corollary}[The real hull has dimension zero; inherited consequence]\label{isg:ptg:cor:dimension}
For $F=\R$, every $k_Y$ in \cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline} has Hausdorff
dimension zero.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
It is a proper analytic real closed subfield. Apply Edgar and Miller's
theorem on such subfields \cite{EdgarMiller}.
\end{proof}

This is stronger than real nullness but is not a new dimension theorem.
Source~04 does not transfer it without proof to $p$-adic Hausdorff
dimension.

\subsection{Arithmetic consequences}
\Cref{isg:ptg:lem:ideal,isg:ptg:lem:analyticss}, together with Glazer's
theorem (\cref{isg:ptg:fact:glazer}), make $\mathcal S=\SSy(M)$ a proper
analytic Turing ideal for every nonstandard Borel $M\models\PA$.
\Cref{isg:ptg:thm:scalar} therefore supplies exactly the hypotheses of
\cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline} for every $\mathcal K_F(\mathcal S)$.

\begin{corollary}[Countable external advice does not close the gap]\label{isg:ptg:cor:advice}
For a nonstandard Borel $M\models\PA$, a countable set $Y\subset F$, and
$F=\R$ or $\Qp$, let
\[
 k_{F,Y}=\acl_F(\mathcal K_F(\SSy(M))(Y)).
\]
Then every nonempty open set in $F$ contains a Cantor set independent over
$k_{F,Y}$, all finite dependence relations over $k_{F,Y}$ are null and
meager, and $\trdeg(F/k_{F,Y})=\cfrak$.
\end{corollary}

Adjoining arbitrary countably many constants is stronger than adjoining
only computable ones; the latter are already in the scalar field. It is
also different from supplying a single oracle that encodes all those
constants and then closing under \emph{all} computations relative to that
oracle. Such a Turing-ideal enlargement is a different construction, not
covered by this field-generation assertion. The claim is about field
generation and relative algebraic closure, as the formula states.

\subsection{One Cantor parameter space for all local fields}
There are countably many places under consideration. They can be treated
simultaneously, without imposing an artificial identification of real and
$p$-adic digits.

\begin{theorem}[Simultaneous local perfect families]\label{isg:ptg:thm:simultaneous}
Fix a nonstandard Borel $M\models\PA$, and use the fields $k_{F,Y}$ of
\cref{isg:ptg:cor:advice}. For each $v\in\{\infty\}\cup\{p:p\text{
prime}\}$, let $F_\infty=\R$, $F_p=\Qp$, and let $Y_v\subset F_v$ be
countable. There are continuous injections
\[
 f_\infty:2^\N\to(1,2),\qquad
 f_p:2^\N\to\Zp\quad(p\text{ prime})
\]
such that, for every place $v$, the image of $f_v$ is algebraically
independent over $k_{F_v,Y_v}$. More generally one can prescribe a nonempty
open real interval and a nonempty open $p$-adic ball at each place.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $U_\infty=(1,2)$ and $U_p=\Zp$, and form the countable product
\[
 \mathcal X=U_\infty\times\prod_p U_p.
\]
It is a perfect Polish space. For each $v,n$, pull back the meager relation
$\Dep_n(k_{F_v,Y_v})\cap U_v^n$ along the coordinate map
$\mathcal X^n\to U_v^n$. This map is continuous, open, and surjective, so
the pullback of a nowhere dense set is nowhere dense and the pullback of a
meager set is meager. Mycielski's theorem supplies a Cantor subset
$\mathcal P\subset\mathcal X$ independent for this countable family of
relations.

Choose a homeomorphism $\eta:2^\N\to\mathcal P$ and set
$f_v=\pi_v\circ\eta$. These maps are continuous. They are injective because
equality of two $v$-coordinates is the polynomial relation $X_1-X_2=0$,
which has been excluded for distinct members of $\mathcal P$. All the other
independence requirements hold by construction. Replacing the factors by
the prescribed open intervals or balls proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The injections need not be computable, and the theorem does not say that
one digit sequence, interpreted unchanged at every place, works. Those are
stronger synchronization requirements. It does give a single compact
parameter space with continuously varying, mutually independent families
at all places.

\section{The residue image of a nonstandard Peano model}\label{isg:ptg:sec:residues}

\subsection{The residue homomorphism}
Let $M$ be a nonstandard model of $\PA$ and $R_M$ its difference ring, the
ring of formal differences of elements of $M$ (\cref{isg:sc:sec:nogo}).
Two pairs represent the same difference when $(a,b)\sim(a',b')$ if
$a+b'=a'+b$. Cancellativity and the ordinary arithmetic identities make
this an integral domain. Only its algebraic structure is needed; no Polish
quotient topology on $R_M$ is assumed.

For every positive standard integer $m$, division with remainder gives
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:finite}
 R_M/mR_M\simeq\Z/m\Z.
\end{equation}
The isomorphism is the unique one carrying standard numerals to their
ordinary residue classes. In particular, the compatible prime-power
remainders give a unital ring homomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:res}
 \res_p:R_M\longrightarrow\varprojlim_n\Z/p^n\Z=\Zp.
\end{equation}
It has kernel $\bigcap_{n\geq1}p^nR_M$. This kernel is nonzero: for a
nonstandard $N\in M$, the nonzero element $p^N$ is divisible by every
standard $p^n$. \textup{[}Added at this write:
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:finite} is the standard division of
\cref{isg:sc:lem:idiv}, which needs only $\Idelta$, read through
\cref{isg:sc:sec:residues}; $\res_p$ is the $p$-adic coordinate of the map
$\res_{R_M}:R_M\to\Zhat$ there.\textup{]}

\subsection{Digits and the standard system}
Fix a bit width $w\geq1$ with $2^w\geq p$. For $z\in\Zp$, write
\[
 z=\sum_{i\geq0}d_i p^i,\qquad 0\leq d_i<p,
\]
and encode each $d_i$ into a block of $w$ binary bits. Write
$\Dig_p(z)\in2^\N$ for the resulting set. Fast $p$-adic names and these
block-digit codes are mutually computable, uniformly for integral inputs.
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:digit}
 z\in\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)\cap\Zp
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \Dig_p(z)\in\mathcal S.
\end{equation}
For the forward direction combine a name-computing oracle with Turing
downward closure; for the reverse, the finite sums of the digit expansion
are a fast name.

\begin{theorem}[Exact residue-image theorem]\label{isg:ptg:thm:residue}
For any nonstandard $M\models\PA$, whether Borel or not, and
$\mathcal S=\SSy(M)$, one has
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:exact}
 \res_p(R_M)=\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)\cap\Zp=:\Zp(\mathcal S),\qquad
 \Frac(\Zp(\mathcal S))=\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S).
\end{equation}
In fact every element of the image is the shadow of an element of $M$, not
merely a formal difference.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $a\in M$, each ordinary $p$-adic digit of $\res_p(a)$ is specified by
an arithmetic formula about division by standard powers of $p$. The
corresponding binary block-digit set is a parameter-definable trace and
hence belongs to $\mathcal S$ by \cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces}. Equation
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:digit} gives $\res_p(a)\in\Zp(\mathcal S)$. Since
$\Zp(\mathcal S)$ is a subring, the same holds for all differences.

Conversely let $z\in\Zp(\mathcal S)$, and choose $c\in M$ whose binary
trace is $\Dig_p(z)$. Internally decode the $i$th block of $w$ bits of $c$,
and reduce that decoded number modulo $p$; call the result $\tilde d_i$. This
reduction ensures $0\leq \tilde d_i<p$ at \emph{all} internal indices, even where
$c$ has no externally prescribed meaning. At every standard $i$, one has
$\tilde d_i=d_i$.

Choose any nonstandard $N\in M$. $\PA$ proves totality of the primitive
recursive finite-sum operation, so it has the internal element
\[
 a=\sum_{i<N}\tilde d_i p^i.
\]
For every standard $n$, reduction of this internal sum modulo $p^n$ leaves
exactly the first $n$ terms, which agree with $z$. Hence $\res_p(a)=z$. No
interchange of an external infinite sum with a ring homomorphism has
occurred: only finite arithmetic, internally evaluated at $N$, and standard
residues have been used.

Finally every $x\in\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ becomes integral after
multiplication by some standard power $p^m$. The scalar $p^mx$ still
belongs to $\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$, so $x=(p^mx)/p^m$ lies in
$\Frac(\Zp(\mathcal S))$. The opposite inclusion holds because
$\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ is a field.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Dense but incomplete residue information]\label{isg:ptg:cor:residuesmall}
If $M$ is nonstandard and Borel, $\Zp(\mathcal S)$ is a proper dense null
meager subring of $\Zp$. It is the valuation ring of
$\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ for the restricted $p$-adic valuation. Its
metric completion is $\Zp$, while the metric completion of
$\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ is $\Qp$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Density follows from the standard integers. Properness follows from
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:exact} and properness of $\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$: if
$\Zp(\mathcal S)=\Zp$, its fraction field would be $\Qp$. Nullness and
meagerness follow by intersecting the null meager field
$\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ with the open set $\Zp$. A valuation ring
consists precisely of the nonnegative-valuation members of its field. The
completion statements follow from density inside the indicated complete
metric spaces.
\end{proof}

There is a sharp distinction between having every \emph{finite} residue
and having every coherent infinite residue sequence. For every $n$, the map
to $\Z/p^n\Z$ is onto; nevertheless, the set of coherent sequences actually
realized by elements of $R_M$ is only $\Zp(\mathcal S)$. Relative algebraic
closure of its fraction field does not repair this topological omission.

\subsection{The full profinite image and its idempotents}\label{isg:ptg:sec:profinite}
The same question has an exact answer simultaneously at all primes. Write
$\Zhat=\varprojlim_m\Z/m\Z\simeq\prod_p\Zp$, with its ordinary compact ring
topology, and let $\res_{R_M}:R_M\to\Zhat$ be the canonical residue map. A
convenient effective representation of $z\in\Zhat$ is the sequence
\[
 r_{n!}(z)\in\{0,\ldots,n!-1\},\qquad r_{n!}(z)\equiv z\pmod{n!}
 \quad(n\geq1).
\]
These residues determine $z$ because the factorial moduli are cofinal for
divisibility. For a Turing ideal $\mathcal S$, put
\[
 \Zhat(\mathcal S)=\{z\in\Zhat:(r_{n!}(z))_{n\geq1}
       \text{ is computable from some }\mathsf A\in\mathcal S\}.
\]
The arithmetic operations on these residue sequences are computable, so
$\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ is a subring. It contains the ordinary integers.

\begin{theorem}[Exact profinite image]\label{isg:ptg:thm:profinite}
For every nonstandard $M\models\PA$ and $\mathcal S=\SSy(M)$,
\[
 \res_{R_M}(R_M)=\res_{R_M}(M)=\Zhat(\mathcal S).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix a primitive recursive injective pairing function $\langle n,j\rangle$
on natural numbers. Since $n!<2^{n^2}$ for $n\geq1$, code the factorial
residue sequence by
\[
 \mathsf A_z=\{\langle n,j\rangle:n\geq1,\ j<n^2,
                   \Bit(r_{n!}(z),j)=1\}.
\]
This code and the residue sequence compute one another. Thus
$z\in\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ exactly when $\mathsf A_z\in\mathcal S$. For
$a\in M$, the code of $\res_{R_M}(a)$ is a parameter-definable trace, so
belongs to $\mathcal S$. Closure under differences gives
$\res_{R_M}(R_M)\subseteq\Zhat(\mathcal S)$.

Conversely, let $z\in\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ and choose $c\in M$ with binary
trace $\mathsf A_z$. For every internal $n\geq1$, define in $M$
\[
 \tilde r_n=\Bigl(\sum_{j<n^2}
       \Bit(c,\langle n,j\rangle)2^j\Bigr)\bmod n!.
\]
For standard $n$, this equals $r_{n!}(z)$. Hence the arithmetic property
\[
 \Phi(N):\quad
 \forall\,1\leq i\leq j\leq N\quad \tilde r_j\equiv \tilde r_i\pmod{i!}
\]
holds for every standard $N$. Overspill supplies a nonstandard $N$ with
$\Phi(N)$. In this elementary use of overspill, if there is a least failure
of $\Phi$, it is nonstandard and its predecessor is a nonstandard success;
if there is no failure, any nonstandard $N$ works. The least-number
principle is available in $\PA$ with parameter $c$.

Set $a=\tilde r_N\in M$. For every standard $n$, the property $\Phi(N)$ gives
$a\equiv r_{n!}(z)\pmod{n!}$. Since every ordinary modulus divides a
factorial, $\res_{R_M}(a)=z$. All coding and summation in this proof are
internal finite arithmetic. Overspill is used here explicitly, rather than
assuming that arbitrary nonstandard residue prefixes are already coherent.
\end{proof}

Enumerate the ordinary primes as $p_0,p_1,\ldots$. For
$\mathsf A\subseteq\N$, let $\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}\in\prod_i\mathbb Z_{p_i}$
have $i$th coordinate $1$ if $i\in\mathsf A$ and $0$ otherwise.

\begin{corollary}[The standard system is the Boolean algebra of idempotents]\label{isg:ptg:cor:idempotents}
With the canonical prime coordinates understood,
\[
 \{e\in\Zhat(\mathcal S):e^2=e\}=\{\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}:\mathsf A\in\mathcal S\}.
\]
This identification is a Boolean-algebra isomorphism, where intersection is
multiplication and complementation is $e\mapsto1-e$. If $\mathcal S$ is
proper, then
\[
 \Zhat(\mathcal S)\ \subsetneq\ \prod_p \Zp(\mathcal S).
\]
If $\mathcal S$ is proper and analytic, $\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ is a proper
analytic dense null meager subring of $\Zhat$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every idempotent in the product has coordinates zero or one, since each
$\Zp$ is an integral domain. The factorial-residue code of
$\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}$ is computable from $\mathsf A$: only finitely many
membership bits are needed for each factorial modulus, and the finite
Chinese remainder algorithm combines them. Conversely, the residue modulo
$p_i$ recovers $\mathsf A(i)$. Consequently
$\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}\in\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ if and only if
$\mathsf A\in\mathcal S$, proving the first assertion and its compatibility
with Boolean operations.

Every coordinate projection of $\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ is $\Zp(\mathcal S)$ by
the two exact image theorems. Choose $\mathsf A\notin\mathcal S$. Each
coordinate of $\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}$ is the ordinary integer zero or one,
so $\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}\in\prod_p\Zp(\mathcal S)$, but
$\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}\notin\Zhat(\mathcal S)$. This proves strictness, not
merely the possibility of strictness.

Analyticity follows by projecting the Borel oracle-computation relation
over $\mathcal S$, as in \cref{isg:ptg:lem:analyticfield}. The ordinary
integers are dense in $\Zhat$ by finite Chinese remainders. A proper
analytic dense subgroup of this compact group is null and meager, by the
same difference-set argument as \cref{isg:ptg:cor:sssmall}.
\end{proof}

The ordinary finite quotients of $\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ itself are also
unchanged. For $m\geq1$, if $x\in\Zhat(\mathcal S)\cap m\Zhat$, the residue
of $x/m$ modulo $n!$ is computed by taking the residue of $x$ modulo $mn!$
and dividing its integer representative by $m$. The representative is
divisible by $m$, and factorial codes compute residues at every ordinary
modulus. Thus $x/m\in\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ and
\[
 m\Zhat(\mathcal S)=\Zhat(\mathcal S)\cap m\Zhat,\qquad
 \Zhat(\mathcal S)/m\Zhat(\mathcal S)\simeq\Z/m\Z.
\]
Its ordinary profinite completion is therefore $\Zhat$. Thus the
distinguished profinite image remembers the complete standard system
through its idempotents, even though its finite quotients and its
completion do not. The coordinatewise product loses the requirement of one
shared oracle, and is strictly larger whenever the standard system is
proper. This answers the full-profinite version of the residue-image
question, rather than leaving simultaneous realization implicit.

\subsection{Canonical residue images of Peano models}\label{isg:ptg:sec:images}
\Cref{isg:sc:q:images} asks which subrings of $\Zhat$ occur as
$\im\res_{R_M}$ for nonstandard $M\models\Idelta$, and records that
\cref{isg:sc:cor:profiles} proves only that the image is not contained in
$\Z$. For models of Peano arithmetic \cref{isg:ptg:thm:profinite} answers
it: the image is $\Zhat(\SSy(M))$, determined by the standard system alone.
The image contains every profinite integer with a computable factorial
residue sequence, because $\SSy(M)$ contains every computable set
(\cref{isg:ptg:lem:ideal}); for instance the parameter $\zeta_*$ of
\eqref{isg:cf:eq:explicitparameter}, whose residues modulo $n!$ are the
computable $S_n$, and which is the residue of the element $F(h)$ in the
proof of \cref{isg:cf:thm:PA}. Saturation
and the choice of model enter only through $\SSy(M)$. The following
statement, added at this write, lists the images that occur among countable
models. A \emph{Scott set} is a nonempty Turing ideal
$\mathcal S\subseteq2^\N$ such that every infinite binary tree coded in
$\mathcal S$ has an infinite path in $\mathcal S$.

\begin{corollary}[Residue images of countable Peano models; \textup{[write~04]}]\label{isg:ptg:cor:images}
Distinct standard systems of nonstandard models of $\PA$ give distinct
residue images. A subring $\Lambda\subseteq\Zhat$ is $\im\res_{R_M}$ for
some countable nonstandard $M\models\PA$ if and only if
$\Lambda=\Zhat(\mathcal S)$ for a countable Scott set $\mathcal S$; and then
$\mathcal S$ is the set of $\mathsf A\subseteq\N$ with
$\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}\in\Lambda$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{isg:ptg:thm:profinite}, $\im\res_{R_M}=\Zhat(\SSy(M))$, and by
the first assertion of \cref{isg:ptg:cor:idempotents}, which uses only that
$\SSy(M)$ is a Turing ideal, the idempotents of this ring are exactly the
$\mathbf 1_{\mathsf A}$ with $\mathsf A\in\SSy(M)$. So the image determines
$\SSy(M)$, which proves the first and the last assertion. The standard
system of every nonstandard model of $\PA$ is a Scott set, and by Scott's
theorem \cite{Scott} every countable Scott set is the standard system of a
countable nonstandard model of $\PA$; both are classical and imported here.
A countable $M$ has a countable standard system. This proves the
equivalence.
\end{proof}

The corollary is the write's combination of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:profinite,isg:ptg:cor:idempotents}
with Scott's theorem; source~04 states neither it nor the Scott-set
description, and says only that it does not claim to solve Scott's general
problem. For uncountable models the images are the rings $\Zhat(\mathcal S)$
of the standard systems that occur; which uncountable Scott sets occur is
Scott's problem, open in general and not addressed here. For models of
$\Idelta$ that are not models of $\PA$ the question stays open: the proofs
of \cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces,isg:ptg:thm:residue,isg:ptg:thm:profinite} use
induction for arbitrary arithmetic formulas, overspill and the totality of
the internal finite sums. See the note after \cref{isg:sc:q:images}.

\subsection{Equal completions, different arithmetic images}\label{isg:ptg:sec:comparison}
The comparison with omnific integers is clearest first in a set-sized ring
where all constructions are elementary. Let $k\subseteq\R$ be a subfield and
put
\begin{align}
 \Pi_k&=\{f\in k((t^\Q)):\supp(f)\subset\Q_{<0}\},\label{isg:ptg:eq:tail}\\
 \Asc(k,\Q)&=\Z+\Pi_k.\label{isg:ptg:eq:tailring}
\end{align}
The support of each Hahn series is well ordered in the increasing order on
$\Q$. In the surreal interpretation $t=\omega^{-1}$, negative $t$-exponents
represent purely infinite terms. There is no requirement that the negative
support be finite. \textup{[}Added at this write: this is source~03's ring
$\Asc(k,\Gamma)$ of \cref{isg:sc:sec:rings} with $\Gamma=\Q$, where the
ring retraction $\ct$ and standard division are already stated.\textup{]}

\begin{proposition}[The ordinary residue tower of $\Asc(k,\Q)$]\label{isg:ptg:prop:tail}
$\Asc(k,\Q)$ is a ring. Constant extraction $\ct:\Asc(k,\Q)\to\Z$ is a
surjective unital ring homomorphism with kernel $\Pi_k$. For every
$n\geq1$,
\[
 \Asc(k,\Q)/n\Asc(k,\Q)\simeq\Z/n\Z,
\]
compatibly with reduction maps. Every homomorphism from $\Asc(k,\Q)$ to a
finite ring annihilates $\Pi_k$ and factors through $\ct$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Sums of negative exponents remain negative, so $\Pi_k$ is closed under
multiplication, and multiplication by an element of $\Z+\Pi_k$ preserves
it. The constant term of a product in $\Asc(k,\Q)$ is exactly the product
of its integer constant terms: no pair of negative exponents can sum to
zero. This proves the ring and homomorphism assertions.

For every nonzero ordinary integer $n$, division of all coefficients by $n$
preserves $\Pi_k$, so $\Pi_k=n\Pi_k$. It follows that
\[
 n\Asc(k,\Q)=n\Z+\Pi_k=\ct^{-1}(n\Z),
\]
which proves the quotient formula. If a finite target ring has
characteristic $m>0$ and $f\in\Pi_k$, write $f=mg$ with $g\in\Pi_k$. Its
image is zero. The factorization is then through the constant-term
quotient. This last argument does not need the homomorphism to be
surjective.
\end{proof}

\textup{[}Added at this write: for the class-sized $\Oz$ these statements
are \replabel{odg:thm:finitequotients} and \cref{isg:cf:prop:omnificresidue},
and they are proved in Lean: \fn{omnificQuotientZModEquiv}
(\fn{OmnificResidues.lean}), and \fn{omnific_finite_hom_kills_purelyInfinite}
and \fn{omnific_finite_hom_factors} (\fn{OmnificFiniteQuotients.lean}),
namespace \fn{Surreal.Foundations.SignSequence}. Under $t=\omega^{-1}$,
$\Asc(\R,\Q)$ is a set-sized subring of $\Oz$ with the same constant term
(\cref{isg:ptg:sec:omnific}); the proposition for $\Asc(k,\Q)$ is not
formalized.\textup{]}

The ordinary $p$-adic completion means the inverse limit over the standard
ideals $p^nR$. It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:samecompletion}
 \widehat{(R_M)}_p\simeq\Zp\simeq\widehat{\Asc(k,\Q)}_p.
\end{equation}
These identifications are ring isomorphisms and homeomorphisms for the
inverse-limit topologies. Nevertheless the canonical images are
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:differentimages}
 \im\bigl(R_M\to\widehat{(R_M)}_p\bigr)=\Zp(\mathcal S),\qquad
 \im\bigl(\Asc(k,\Q)\to\widehat{\Asc(k,\Q)}_p\bigr)=\Z.
\end{equation}
For example,
\[
 \frac1{p+1}\in\Zp(\mathcal S)\setminus\Z,
\]
because it is a rational $p$-adic integer and is computable. Therefore
these two images are different even when both completions and all the
finite stages are identified canonically.

\begin{theorem}[Finite completion does not determine the canonical image]\label{isg:ptg:thm:completions}
A signed nonstandard $\PA$ ring $R_M$ and the omnific-type ring $\Asc(k,\Q)$
have identical ordinary finite residue systems and identical ordinary
$p$-adic completions, but their canonical images in those completions are
different. If $R_M$ comes from a Borel model, its image has the proper
analytic fraction field of \cref{isg:ptg:thm:scalar}; the image of
$\Asc(k,\Q)$ is always the ordinary integers.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Combine \eqref{isg:ptg:eq:finite}, \cref{isg:ptg:prop:tail} and
\cref{isg:ptg:thm:residue}. The transition maps are all the ordinary
residue reductions, so the inverse limits agree as claimed.
Equation~\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:differentimages} and the exhibited rational
element separate the images.
\end{proof}

\textup{[}Added at this write: for $\Oz$ the second half is
\replabel{odg:eq:profinite} and is proved in Lean: the canonical maps to
the $p$-adic and to the profinite completion are constant extraction
followed by the ordinary embedding of $\Z$ (\fn{omnificPadicCompletionEquiv_of}
in \fn{OmnificPadicCompletion.lean}, \fn{omnificProfiniteCompletionEquiv_of}
in \fn{OmnificProfiniteCompletion.lean}). The first half is
\cref{isg:ptg:thm:residue}. The theorem is the exact Peano form of the
remark after \cref{isg:sc:prop:diagonal}: neither the finite quotients nor
the abstract inverse limit separate omnific from nonstandard arithmetic,
and the canonical image does.\textup{]}

\begin{corollary}[A useful nonidentification]\label{isg:ptg:cor:nomap}
There is no unital ring homomorphism $R_M\to\Z$, and hence none
$R_M\to\Asc(k,\Q)$. This holds for every nonstandard $\PA$ model, without
the Borel hypothesis.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A unital homomorphism $h:R_M\to\Z$ preserves each standard remainder: from
$d=p^nq+r$ it follows that $h(d)\equiv r\pmod{p^n}$. Therefore $\res_p(d)$
would equal the ordinary integer $h(d)$ inside $\Zp$, forcing
$\Zp(\mathcal S)\subseteq\Z$, a contradiction. Composing a hypothetical map
to $\Asc(k,\Q)$ with $\ct$ gives the forbidden map to $\Z$.
\end{proof}

\textup{[}Added at this write: \cref{isg:sc:thm:nochar} proves more,
for every nonstandard model $M$ of $\Idelta$, namely
$\Hom_\Z((R_M)_{\rm add},\Z)=0$; so there is no additive map $R_M\to\Z$ sending $1$ to $1$, and by
\cref{isg:sc:cor:noadd,isg:sc:cor:omnific} none into $\Asc(k,\Q)$ or $\Oz$.
The proof above is a second route to the ring-homomorphism case for
Peano models, through the residue image: it is the criterion of
\cref{isg:sc:prop:diagonal}, applied with the explicit non-diagonal
residue $1/(p+1)$.\textup{]}

This corollary is included to prevent an incorrect interpretation of the
bridge. Source~04 does \emph{not} embed the original $\PA$ ring into the
omnific integers. It transports algebraic information about the
\emph{scalar field extracted from its standard system}. The two routes must
not be conflated.

\section{Hahn coefficients and an invisible omnific family}\label{isg:ptg:sec:hahn}

\subsection{Coefficientwise independence in Hahn fields}
For a field $k$ and an ordered abelian set-group $\Gamma$, the Hahn field
$k((t^\Gamma))$ consists of formal sums
\[
 f=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}a_\gamma t^\gamma
\]
with well-ordered support. Equality is coefficientwise. Addition is
coefficientwise, and multiplication is the usual well-defined convolution.
If $k\subseteq k'$, the series with coefficients in $k$ form the subfield
$k((t^\Gamma))$ of $k'((t^\Gamma))$. Only standard Hahn arithmetic is used;
no topology on the collection of all possible coefficient fields is needed.

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient-preserving independence transfer]\label{isg:ptg:thm:hahn}
Let $k\subseteq k'$ be fields and $\Gamma$ an ordered abelian set-group. If
$P\subseteq k'$ is algebraically independent over $k$, its constant images
in $k'((t^\Gamma))$ are algebraically independent over $k((t^\Gamma))$.
More generally, for any choices $u_r\in k((t^\Gamma))^\times$ indexed by
$r\in P$, the family $\{u_rr:r\in P\}$ is algebraically independent over
$k((t^\Gamma))$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take distinct $r_1,\ldots,r_m\in P$ and a polynomial
\[
 Q(X_1,\ldots,X_m)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal E}a_\nu X^\nu,
 \qquad a_\nu\in k((t^\Gamma)),
\]
where $\mathcal E\subseteq\N^m$ is finite. Suppose $Q(\bar r)=0$. Write
$a_\nu=\sum_\gamma a_{\nu,\gamma}t^\gamma$. The coefficient of $t^\gamma$
in the assumed equality is
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:hahncoefficient}
 \sum_{\nu\in\mathcal E}a_{\nu,\gamma}\,\bar r^{\nu}=0.
\end{equation}
This is a polynomial relation over $k$. Independence of the $r_i$ forces
$a_{\nu,\gamma}=0$ for every $\nu$ and every $\gamma$. Hence every
$a_\nu=0$ and $Q=0$.

Only a finite outer sum was used in \eqref{isg:ptg:eq:hahncoefficient};
this is not an illicit exchange of arbitrary infinite sums. For the final
assertion, substitution $X_i\mapsto u_{r_i}X_i$ is an automorphism of the
polynomial ring over $k((t^\Gamma))$, with inverse
$X_i\mapsto u_{r_i}^{-1}X_i$. It preserves nonzero polynomials and reduces
the assertion to the constant case.
\end{proof}

\textup{[}Added at this write: the first assertion is an immediate
consequence of the coefficient-extension lemma of the
independent-surreal-copies report, \replabel{isc:cp:lem:coeffld}: for
$k\subseteq k'$, the fields $k'$ and $k((t^\Gamma))$ are linearly disjoint
over $k$ inside $k'((t^\Gamma))$, in the same small-$t$ convention. The
distinct monomials in $P$ are linearly independent over $k$, hence over
$k((t^\Gamma))$, which is algebraic independence. The proof above is the
same coefficient comparison.\textup{]}

There is no bound on the cardinality or complexity of the supports beyond
the set-sized Hahn definition. The theorem therefore identifies an exact
limitation of support enrichment: introducing more monomials and longer
well-ordered supports cannot create a polynomial relation between scalar
constants whose coefficients are still restricted to $k$.

\begin{corollary}[Persistent real coefficient gap]\label{isg:ptg:cor:hahnarith}
For a nonstandard Borel $M\models\PA$, countable $Y\subseteq\R$, and
$k_{\R,Y}=\acl_\R(\mathcal K_\infty(\SSy(M))(Y))$, choose a Cantor set
$P\subset(1,2)$ independent over $k_{\R,Y}$. Then for every ordered abelian
set-group $\Gamma$, the same $P$ is independent over
$k_{\R,Y}((t^\Gamma))$ inside $\R((t^\Gamma))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use \cref{isg:ptg:cor:advice,isg:ptg:thm:hahn}.
\end{proof}

A corresponding complex-coefficient statement also holds. If $P$ is real
and independent over $k'\subseteq\R$, it is independent over $k'(i)$:
split a putative relation into real and imaginary parts.
\Cref{isg:ptg:thm:hahn} then gives independence over $k'(i)((t^\Gamma))$ in
$\C((t^\Gamma))$. This is the relevant surcomplex analogue; it makes no
assertion about independence over the whole surcomplex field.

\subsection{A bounded-support route into the surreals}\label{isg:ptg:sec:omnific}
For rational exponents, Conway normal forms give the familiar embedding
\begin{equation}\label{isg:ptg:eq:surrealmap}
 \R((t^\Q))\longrightarrow\No,
 \qquad \sum_q a_q t^q\longmapsto\sum_q a_q\omega^{-q}.
\end{equation}
Increasing well-ordered $t$-support becomes decreasing well-ordered Conway
support in the usual reverse-order convention. Normal-form addition and
multiplication agree with Hahn arithmetic. The identification between
surreal normal forms and Hahn-style arithmetic is also part of the ProveIt
development consulted by source~04 \cite{ProveItPTG}; a primary modern
treatment is \cite{BKMM}.

Under \eqref{isg:ptg:eq:surrealmap}, $\Asc(\R,\Q)=\Z+\Pi_\R$ is a set-sized
subring of Conway's omnific integers: all nonconstant growth exponents are
positive and the constant coefficient is an ordinary integer. In particular
$r\omega$ is an omnific integer for every $r\in\R$.

\begin{theorem}[An invisible independent family at one scale]\label{isg:ptg:thm:omnific}
Let $M$, $\mathcal S$, $Y$, $k_{\R,Y}$ and $P$ be as in
\cref{isg:ptg:cor:hahnarith}, with $\Gamma=\Q$. The family
\[
 \mathcal Z=\{z_r=r\omega:r\in P\}
\]
has cardinality $\cfrak$, is algebraically independent over the embedded
field $k_{\R,Y}((t^\Q))$, and has the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\arabic*)}]
\item Every $z_r$ is purely infinite and lies in the set-sized ring
$\Asc(\R,\Q)\subset\Oz$.
\item For every positive ordinary integer $n$, every $z_r$ belongs to
$n\Asc(\R,\Q)$ and therefore maps to zero in $\Oz/n\Oz$.
\item Every homomorphism from $\Asc(\R,\Q)$ to a finite ring kills the
whole family. Nevertheless, for each finite distinct tuple
$z_{r_1},\ldots,z_{r_m}$, evaluation gives an injective homomorphism
\[
 k_{\R,Y}((t^\Q))[X_1,\ldots,X_m]\hookrightarrow\R((t^\Q)).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use \cref{isg:ptg:thm:hahn} with $u_r=t^{-1}=\omega$. Each element has
negative $t$-support $\{-1\}$ and zero constant term. Since
$z_r/n=(r/n)\omega\in\Asc(\R,\Q)$, it is divisible by every ordinary $n$.
The finite-image assertion is \cref{isg:ptg:prop:tail}. The evaluation map
is injective exactly because the tuple is algebraically independent over
the specified coefficient field.
\end{proof}

The coefficient field is essential. Over the full real field, two nonzero
members $r\omega$ and $s\omega$ satisfy $sX_1-rX_2=0$. This does not
contradict the theorem because those coefficients need not belong to
$k_{\R,Y}((t^\Q))$. The statement neither claims independence over $\R$
nor mistakes algebraic independence for independence of distinct valuation
scales: all these elements use the \emph{same} scale $\omega$.
\textup{[}Added at this write: each $z_r$ is, separately, transcendental
over $\R$, as every infinite omnific integer is (Lean
\fn{omnific_transcendental_of_not_finite} in
\fn{OmnificPolynomialRoots.lean}); what fails over $\R$ is the joint
independence. By \replabel{odg:thm:finitequotients} every element of
$\Pi$, not only these, is zero in every $\Oz/n\Oz$.\textup{]}

\subsection{Which topology has the perfect set?}\label{isg:ptg:sec:topologies}
The Cantor structure lives on $P\subset\R$ and may be transported to
$\mathcal Z$ as a coefficient-parameter topology. It is not the topology
induced by the order on $\No$. If $r\ne s$ are real, then $|r-s|\omega$
exceeds every positive ordinary integer. Consequently
\[
 (r\omega-1,r\omega+1)\cap\mathcal Z=\{r\omega\}.
\]
Thus $\mathcal Z$ is discrete in the surreal order topology. The same
warning applies to the Hahn valuation topology: distinct nonzero constant
differences have valuation zero, and the common monomial factor does not
turn the real coefficient topology into that topology.

This is why ``Cantor-parameterized omnific family'' is the accurate phrase.
The real coefficient set is perfect; the omnific image carries several
inequivalent natural topologies, and perfectness is not invariant under
changing them.

\subsection{Set theory without class paradoxes}\label{isg:ptg:sec:classes}
All fields, models, countable parameter sets, Cantor sets, and Hahn
supports used in the main proofs are sets. For $\Gamma=\Q$ the domain of
\eqref{isg:ptg:eq:surrealmap} is a set; its images are set-coded surreal
sign sequences, and their birthdays have an ordinal bound by Replacement.
One can therefore perform the entire application in a sufficiently large
set-sized initial segment of the ambient class of surreal numbers, without
treating the proper class $\No$ as an element of itself.

In a class theory, \eqref{isg:ptg:eq:surrealmap} is simply a class-defined
map on a set domain. In a universe-polymorphic type theory, the same proof
should first be stated for a set-sized Hahn field and an embedding into a
larger universe. Neither a global well-order of all surreals nor
quantification over a ``set of all class embeddings'' is needed. The
general coefficient theorem is a scheme over set-sized $\Gamma$, not a Hahn
construction whose exponent group is assumed to be a set while it is
actually the whole proper class $\No$. This is the same discipline as
\cref{isg:sec:background} and \cref{isg:sc:sec:classes}.

\subsection{Scope, sharpness, and what is not constructed}\label{isg:ptg:sec:limits}
\paragraph{Nonstandardness cannot simply be removed.} For the standard
model $M=\N$, binary traces of single integers are only finite sets. They
are not a Turing ideal containing all computable sets. Its signed ring is
$\Z$ and its residue image is $\Z\subset\Zp$, not the valuation ring of all
computable $p$-adic numbers. Thus the nonstandard hypothesis is already
necessary for the exact coding bridge as stated.

\paragraph{Topological and algebraic closure point in opposite
directions.} For a Borel nonstandard model, $\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$ is
algebraically closed relative to $\Qp$ but is not metrically complete. Its
topological completion is all of $\Qp$, whereas adjoining countably many
constants and taking relative algebraic closure still leaves a perfect
transcendence gap. The completion adds limits of arbitrary Cauchy
sequences, not merely roots of polynomials or values of finitely many field
operations. Similarly, allowing every Hahn support with coefficients in
$k_{\R,Y}$ is not the same operation as replacing $k_{\R,Y}$ by its
topological completion.

\paragraph{No named transcendental witness is supplied.} Every computable
real belongs to $\mathcal K_\infty(\mathcal S)$, and every computable
$p$-adic point belongs to $\mathcal K_p(\mathcal S)$. Consequently no member
of the independent sets can have a computable name. In particular the
theorem does not produce a new independence result about a pair of
familiar computable constants. It produces independence \emph{over a field
already containing all such constants}.

There is also no computable injection from all of Cantor space into one of
the independent sets: evaluating it at the computable branch $0^\N$ would
give a computable point of that set, a contradiction. A continuous
injection exists by \cref{isg:ptg:thm:simultaneous}, but a code for it
carries additional noncomputable information. The proof is existential,
not a finite approximation algorithm for the full perfect family.

\paragraph{No global theory of arbitrary presentations is assumed.} The
scalar field depends only on the standard system, not on a selected Polish
topology on the presentation of $M$. The Borel hypothesis is used to obtain
analyticity and properness, and not to select canonical oracles for
elements. The argument also does not require continuous arithmetic
operations, a Polish topology on the signed quotient ring, or a Borel
global root-selection function. All such stronger assertions would need
separate proofs.

\section{Verification, provenance, and a formalization route}\label{isg:sec:audit}

Each subsection below first gives source~01's record, as delivered, and
then those of sources~02, 03 and~04; ``this manuscript'' in source~01's text means source~01.

\subsection{The exact dependency chain}
The proof separates into three kinds of input. Standard surreal
normal forms identify the ambient additive spaces. The established
Ehrlich--Kaplan criterion decides when a specified canonical Hahn image
is initial. Everything connecting the original discrete group to the
normalized image is then given by the explicit formulas and proofs in
this manuscript.

The essential chain is
\begin{gather*}
 \text{bottom coefficient structure}
 \ \longrightarrow\ \text{root relocation}\\
 \ \longrightarrow\ \text{strong Hahn transport}
 \ \longrightarrow\ \text{initiality of the image}.
\end{gather*}
For the inverse classification, the additional chain is
\begin{gather*}
 \text{missing ancestors of }p
 \ \longrightarrow\ \text{spine exponents}\\
 \ \longrightarrow\ \text{forced dyadic coefficients}
 \ \longrightarrow\ K_\alpha\subseteq H.
\end{gather*}
The sign-sequence proof is independent of the arithmetic of exponents.
In particular, no unproved assertion that $\tht$ preserves addition of
exponents occurs in the chain.

\emph{Source~02.} The convex-factor theorem depends on normal forms and
the initial Hahn criterion, then only on the proved projection and sign
lemmas. The Presburger criterion adds the divisible initial-embedding
theorem (\cref{isg:cf:fact:ehrlich}). The explicit nonsplit examples add
no further structural input. The elementary-equivalence consequences use
the classical characterization of Presburger models; the ultrapower
statement additionally uses \L o\'s's theorem. The Peano statement uses
the usual provably total factorial and summation recursions. The
surcomplex module assertions are coordinatewise consequences of the real
factor theorem. The merge statements
\cref{isg:cf:prop:cyclic,isg:cf:cor:limit,isg:cf:cor:periods} add
source~01's \cref{isg:sec:surgery,isg:sec:quotient} and, for the last,
statements of the trigonometry report cited by label.

\emph{Source~03.} The detector (\cref{isg:sc:thm:dyadic}) uses only
discreteness, ordinary division by $2$ and $3$, and a normalized additive
map into the actual ordinary integers; its inductiveness
(\cref{isg:sc:lem:successor}) uses only division by $2$. The character
classification adds division by every ordinary integer; the no-map theorem
adds bounded induction (\cref{isg:sc:lem:idiv}); the compiler adds an
actually positive infinite bound; the complexity theorem adds an effective
presentation and a specified code for that bound, but no effectiveness of
the character. The open-induction example imports the classical
integer-part theorem (\cref{isg:sc:sec:shepherdson}). The
\textup{[write]} statements of \cref{isg:sc:sec:fragment} add source~02's
\cref{isg:cf:main:presburger,isg:cf:prop:residuesplit,isg:cf:cor:unit}, and
\cref{isg:sc:rem:ELV} adds \cite[Theorem~2.9]{ELV}.

Source~03 lists hypotheses that must not be silently weakened. The
detector needs a character with values in the \emph{ordinary} integers: a
map into a nonstandard copy of arithmetic does not support the external
$2$-adic case split used in the proof. Standard division means externally
indexed division by every ordinary modulus; the detector itself needs only
$2$ and $3$. A unit-preserving additive map is stronger than an arbitrary
additive embedding, and no theorem here excludes every additive embedding
without the unit condition. The character theorem uses standard division
to normalize a nonzero character; the uniqueness replacement
(\cref{isg:sc:prop:repair}), in contrast, needs only division by $2$. The
truth compiler assumes an actually positive infinite bound. Its uniformity
is over formulas, rings, and such bounds; no computable test for
infinitude in arbitrary presentations is asserted. An effective
presentation and a specified element code are needed for the upper
many-one reduction.

\emph{Source~04.} The perfect-gap theorem
(\cref{isg:ptg:thm:headline}) depends on the analytic-derivation lemma
(\cref{isg:ptg:lem:derivation}: extension of a derivation through a
separable algebraic extension, the analytic graph, and automatic continuity
of Borel homomorphisms), on Fubini and Kuratowski--Ulam for the aggregate
relations, and on Mycielski's theorem, in the form of
\cite[Theorem~1]{DolezalKubis}. The arithmetic applications add
\cref{isg:ptg:lem:traces,isg:ptg:lem:ideal,isg:ptg:lem:analyticss}, which
use full induction, Glazer's theorem (\cref{isg:ptg:fact:glazer}),
imported, and quantifier elimination for real closed fields and for $\Qp$
\cite{Macintyre}, imported. The exact images
(\cref{isg:ptg:thm:residue,isg:ptg:thm:profinite}) use only definable
traces, internal finite sums and, for the profinite image, an explicit
overspill; they need no Borel hypothesis. The Hahn and omnific statements
use coefficient comparison and the rational Hahn slice of the surreal
normal forms. The real dimension corollary imports Edgar and Miller's
theorem \cite{EdgarMiller} and is not used elsewhere. Source~04's
dependency and novelty ledger, its Appendix~B, reads as follows with this
report's numbers (its shipped \fn{04-transcendence-gaps-theorem_ledger.md}
is a longer version with source~04's numbers).
\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.32\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Claim & Principal dependency & Status \\
\midrule
\endhead
No full Borel standard system & Glazer's seminar theorem & External input, not reproved (\cref{isg:ptg:fact:glazer}). \\
Scalar-field algebraic closure & Oracle root computation & Proved, using standard effective local methods (\cref{isg:ptg:thm:scalar}). \\
No positive countable transcendence degree & Analytic graph of a derivation; automatic continuity & Full proof; priority not certified (\cref{isg:ptg:lem:derivation}). \\

Hahn coefficient transfer & Coefficient equality and finite polynomial sums & Full elementary proof (\cref{isg:ptg:thm:hahn}). \\

\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\noindent The \textup{[write~04]} corollary (\cref{isg:ptg:cor:images})
adds the exact profinite image, the Boolean reconstruction and Scott's
theorem \cite{Scott}, imported.

\subsection{Finite regression checks supplied with the article}
The accompanying Python program \texttt{verify.py} (shipped in this
report as \fn{code/verify.py}) uses only the standard
library, exact rational arithmetic, and a fixed random seed. Its recorded
run performs \textbf{450,862 assertions}, all passing (shipped as
\fn{data/verification_report.json}). The tests cover
finite sign words through length seven for all relevant finite cone
minima; every prefix-closed subset of the binary tree through depth
three, including the empty tree; inverse-spine tests on a further family
of nonnegative initial trees; and 1,500 random pairs of finite rational
normal forms.

The count of prefix-closed trees is independently specified by
\[
 T_{-1}=1,\qquad T_d=1+T_{d-1}^2,
\]
so $T_0=2$, $T_1=5$, $T_2=26$, and $T_3=677$.
The recurrence counts the empty tree, or a root with independently
chosen initial left and right subtrees. This makes the finite-tree
coverage exhaustive at the stated depth, rather than a random sample.

Tests check inverse identities, order preservation, the exact predecessor
identity, the right-ancestor
identity away from the minimum, loss of the minimum's right ancestors,
initiality of transported trees, the exact inverse exponent criterion,
and the source dyadic obligations recovered by adjoining the spine.
The series tests check inverse transport, additivity, order, truncations,
and integrality of the bottom coefficient. Separate negative tests reject
the false global right-ancestor identity and unrestricted inversion.

The program does not represent arbitrary ordinals, arbitrary real
coefficients, or infinite Hahn supports. It does not check the imported
characterization theorem or establish absence of a transfinite proof gap.
Its role is regression testing of the finite combinatorics and coefficient
formulas. The written proofs, not these experiments, carry the general
claims.

When this report was placed, the program was rerun on a copy of the
directory with an explicit output file. It passed with the same 450,862
assertions, and its JSON output was identical in content to the shipped
record. The program's default output file is
\fn{verification_report.json} in the current directory, and the
delivered build script calls it with that name, so a rerun should always
be made on a copy (the collection README gives the commands).

\emph{Source~02.} Its program, shipped as
\fn{code/02-convex-factors-verify.py} (delivered as
\fn{code/verify.py}), uses only the Python standard library and exact
integers and rational fractions. Its recorded run, shipped as
\fn{data/02-convex-factors-verification.json} (delivered as
\fn{data/verification.json}), returns \texttt{PASS}. The main coverage
is:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.72\textwidth}r@{}}
\toprule
Check family & Count\\
\midrule
All nonempty convex intervals in the sign tree of lengths $\le5$ & 2,016\\
All nonempty meet-closed subsets of the sign tree of lengths $\le2$ & 61\\
Pairs checked for order, prefix reflection, and meet preservation & 1,398,609\\
Nested convex interval pairs in the sign tree of lengths $\le4$ & 46,376\\
Points checked for two-stage compression coherence & 324,632\\
Factorial transition identities & 100\\
Compatibility checks between factorial residues & 5,050\\
Integer-parameter splitting identities & 1,769\\
Finite projection composition identities & 24,300\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
No row certifies a transfinite theorem. The program additionally checks
initiality of every finite compressed image (145,740 prefix checks), the
non-meet-closed collision example, the solvability of all finite
retraction systems through sixty stages, the factorial bound used in
\cref{isg:cf:lem:noninteger}, and additivity of interval projections
(2,970 checks); the full counts are in the JSON record. The successful
finite retractions are a deliberate regression case: the code must not
mistake finite solvability for a global splitting. The tests do not
verify the Ehrlich--Kaplan theorem, arbitrary ordinals, class
comprehension, completeness of Presburger arithmetic, recursive
saturation, or the Peano-model argument. Those depend on the written
proofs and the explicitly imported results, not on the test counts.

The program prints its JSON report and writes a file only when
\texttt{--output} is given; the delivered README's rerun command names the
delivered record as that output and would overwrite it, so it too should
be rerun on a copy. At this write it was rerun on a copy with a scratch
output file: it passed in about ten seconds, and the JSON content was
identical to the shipped record.

The two Python programs use only the standard library. The first performs

evaluation kernels of \cref{isg:sc:prop:correction}. The second implements
a finite syntax tree and the single-bound compiler; its audit checks that
every output quantifier has bound $\Omega$ (written \texttt{H}), that no
free variable other than $\Omega$ remains for sentence inputs, and that
every atom has total degree at most two. Local gate evaluations are checked

Neither program evaluates all quantifiers in an infinite interval, checks
all models, or constitutes a proof-assistant verification. The universal
claims rest on the proofs; the code checks algebraic certificates and the
concrete implementation of syntax transformations. The compiler takes
formula trees through its constructors; it does not parse arbitrary
strings or evaluate quantified formulas at infinite bounds. At this write
both programs were rerun on a copy with scratch output files (Python
3.14.4): they passed in about two seconds and under one second, and both
JSON outputs were identical in content to the shipped records. The
programs' default output files are \fn{verification.json} and
\fn{compiler_report.json} in the current directory, so a rerun should be
made on a copy or with an explicit \texttt{--output}. The shipped

pages, no overfull boxes or unresolved references), which is not shipped.
